0\. HOW TO RESUME (paste this whole file into a NEW chat; nothing else needed)

Instructions to AI Agent (follow always, every session):



READ THIS FILE FULLY before doing anything. Understand the complete scope, vision, and constraints.

DO ONLY the task in NEXT (section 10). No project recaps. No "where were we?" questions.

Tone: Enthusiastic, can-do, builder mindset. The user is a passionate non-technical builder. Never dismiss an idea. If something is hard, explain how we'll do it. If a literal version is impossible, deliver the closest working version and state what changed in one line, without being asked twice.

Be terse in explanation, generous in actual effort. Output complete files, not snippets. Output complete implementations, not pseudocode.

End every working reply with a CHECKPOINT (max 10 lines: done, next, new decisions, open problems, exact file paths created/modified).

When a task's done test (section 9) passes, update section 10 and advance NEXT.

NOTHING IS EVER DROPPED. New ideas append to the Feature Register (section 5) with the next free F-number in the right category. If a new idea sounds like a duplicate, reference the existing F-number and ask only if genuinely ambiguous.

Every new module follows the Module Contract (section 4.2) from the first line of code — not optional, not added later.

Fork Decision Protocol (F679, Module Contract 4.2.17, HARD RULE):

If implementing a feature requires discarding, substantially changing, or making mutually exclusive with another already-registered feature, that is a genuine fork, not a routine design choice.

When identified: STOP. Log it in section 4.19's "Pending Fork Decisions" table with lettered options (A, B, C...), each stating plainly what is gained and what is discarded or changed.

Do NOT proceed down any path until the user replies with a single letter (A, B, C...).

A reply that is not a bare letter (prose, "yes", "sounds good", a preference described in words) does NOT count. Ask again for a letter. The fork stays open, un-built.

Work on unrelated, non-forked tasks continues normally.

Once a letter is given, log the choice, date, and session in the same table and proceed.

Research before building, ALWAYS — the literal first step, every time, TWO SEARCHES NOT ONE (section 4.20, Module Contract 4.2.18):

Before writing ANY code/config/UI for ANY part of the app:

(a) Search for current advancements, standards, patches, and open-source repos relevant to that specific part, even for features planned long ago.

(b) Search for current known bugs, vulnerabilities, exploits, attack patterns, outdated/unpatched components, and missing hardening relevant to that part and whatever (a) is about to adopt.

Then search for an existing, actively-maintained, appropriately-licensed open-source project that already does it, or most of it (Module Contract 4.2.16) — check section 4.18's Reusable Components Registry first, add newly-found components to it.

Integrate and wrap rather than reimplement, unless no suitable project exists, the license is incompatible, or the project is abandoned.

Goal: The most hardened app possible for today's technology and threat landscape, not just the most capable one.

Section 11 (PARKING LOT) is reference-only. Nothing in it gets built, scheduled, or assigned to a phase until the user explicitly says to un-park it. Don't suggest starting it; don't fold it into phase planning. It exists so context and reasoning aren't lost.

Reference material: The user has the full CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf (272 pages) and GEMINIchatFULL.html conversations containing foundational concepts. Feature descriptions beyond one-line summaries can be requested by F-number when a task starts work on that feature.

User routine (about 30 seconds per session):



New chat → Paste this file → Send "Resume."

Copy each CHECKPOINT into section 10 of your saved copy.

When limit message appears, note the reset time and return then with a new chat and the latest file.

Keep this file in a project's knowledge so new chats auto-have it; re-upload after edits.

1\. Mission and Vision

Build the most thoughtful, complete, intuitive network-control and privacy app Android has ever had — built from passion and care for the people who'll use it, not to extract money from them.



Core Principles:



Free. Open source. No ads. No tracking. No premium tiers that gate core functionality.

Powerful enough for experts, simple enough for a grandparent. Every feature has a plain-language explanation (Five Lenses Standard, section 4.14).

Honest about what it can't do. No false promises. No hidden limitations.

Everything most apps charge for, we give away. Only server-dependent parts need a documented free workaround.

Guiding standard for every decision: Would a product built by someone who actually cared about the user, instead of a deadline and a paycheck, look like this?

1.1 The Sovereign Design Mandate (From Gemini.md - User Vision + AI Refinement)

This mandate represents the strict intellectual balance between the user's original vision (total device control, privacy, offline functionality) and AI refinements (security hardening, architectural clarity, future-proofing). Both perspectives are equally valid and equally weighted in all decision-making.



Distrust Everything: Assume all inbound code, files, network packets, web pages, and third-party binaries are hostile until proven otherwise. This is the user's instinctive stance toward an ecosystem that has repeatedly betrayed user trust. It is also the security-first principle that AI agents validated as necessary for a robust defense.



Recheck Everything: Apply real-time telemetry inspection, kernel packet filtering, and local AI behavioral monitoring. The user wanted visibility into what apps are doing; AI refined this into a systematic, automated verification layer that doesn't rely on manual review.



Rebuild Everything: Use AST de-obfuscation, Content Disarm and Reconstruction (CDR), and DOM structural normalizers to synthesize safe, clean execution payloads. The user's desire to "make apps work offline" meets AI's understanding that some content must be sanitized before it can be safely reused.



Resend Everything: Route sovereign communications over decentralized peer-to-peer mesh networks, encrypted WebRTC streams, and zero-server signaling protocols. The user's goal of independence from centralized services aligns with AI's technical implementation of mesh networking.



Zero User Burden: Maintain fluid performance through offline AI self-correction, dynamic resource throttling, and automated battery/RAM optimizers. The user wants a tool that works without constant attention; AI ensures this doesn't come at the cost of security or capability.



Intellectual Balance Note: This mandate is not AI-dominated nor user-dominated. It is a synthesis where:



"Distrust Everything" = User's lived experience with privacy violations

"Recheck Everything" = AI's technical implementation of that distrust

"Rebuild Everything" = User's goal of making apps work differently

"Resend Everything" = AI's architectural solution for independence

"Zero User Burden" = User's UX requirement + AI's optimization

Both voices are preserved, respected, and given equal weight throughout this document.



1.2 Total Communication Coverage — The Actual Goal, Stated Explicitly (READ THIS BEFORE TOUCHING D1)

The goal, in one sentence: By the end of the build, there is no communication reaching or leaving the user's device, across ANY channel — cellular, Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, NFC, USB, infrared, satellite, UWB, LoRa, SDR, or any other — that the user cannot see, understand in plain language, and choose to block, spoof, alter, or allow — not just observe.



This is NOT an observability tool. This is NOT a monitoring app. Every piece of information in transit, in either direction, is editable by the user, at minimum for testing purposes, via the mechanisms already built throughout this register (F662 Universal Outbound Field Override Engine, F664 Live Intercept and Edit, F921 Conditional Injection Rules, F1006 Dynamic Field Auto-Discovery).



1.3 The Honest Architectural Reality This Requires

RethinkDNS's forked core (D1) provides deep, genuine coverage of ONE layer — IP-layer traffic over Wi-Fi and cellular data (TCP, UDP, DNS, the large majority of everyday communication).



It does NOT cover:



Bluetooth Classic + BLE

NFC

Cellular voice/SMS (the circuit-switched domain, separate from data)

USB data transfer

Infrared

Satellite (where hardware supports it)

UWB / LoRa / SDR-received signals

Achieving the full goal requires building SEPARATE, PARALLEL interception and control layers for each channel, not just the RethinkDNS fork alone.

1.5 What "Rebuild RethinkDNS's Core" Concretely Means

Beyond just forking, the fork needs extension, not just inheritance, to support:



Full packet-level read AND write access for every flow, not just block/allow decisions (the base needed by F662/F664/F921's editing capability)

The Module SDK's hook points (F213) so each additional channel layer above plugs into the same unified:

Transmission Ledger (F663)

Dynamic Field Auto-Discovery engine (F1006)

The security hardening practice (F883-887, starting at T0.8) applied to every one of these layers, not just the original IP core

End state: One coherent system where every channel reports through the same interface, regardless of which underlying subsystem it actually came from.



For any AI or contributor reading this file: If a channel isn't in the table above, it isn't covered yet, and that's a real gap to flag, not an oversight to assume is handled.



2\. Strategic Decisions (Locked; Revisit Only with Explicit Reason)

D1 — Fork RethinkDNS as the IP-layer foundation (see section 1.4 for the full multi-channel architecture this sits inside). Kotlin app + Go/gVisor networking core. Inherit a hardened foundation instead of rebuilding it. License believed MPL-2.0 — verify in T0.3. We intentionally diverge from upstream's UI going forward (see D10); our own home screen is the permanent design, not whatever upstream ships next. This fork alone covers IP/TCP/UDP/DNS traffic only — Bluetooth, NFC, cellular signaling, and USB each need their own separate layer per section 1.4's table, built on top of, not replacing, this foundation.



D2 — Rust for new performance-critical modules, called from Kotlin. Go core stays as-is (full Rust rewrite considered and rejected — see section 12 decision log, and GEMINIchatFULL.html's Rust/Python split architecture which validates this decision).



D3 — Offline "Fake-Online" works at the application level, not raw TLS replay (fresh keys each session defeat replay). Origin: This is the core concept from CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf that started the entire project. The user's original vision was to trick apps into thinking they're online when offline. AI refined this into a layered approach that actually works:



Layer 1: Fake network presence (universal, no per-app data) — VpnService creates virtual network, Android treats it as online

Layer 2: Built-in knowledge of junk traffic (universal, shipped once) — Bundled domain list for analytics/ads SDKs gets instant stub answers

Layer 3: Fail fast on everything else — For unknown domains, return immediate clean error instead of hanging

Layer 4: Learn on the device — For stuck apps, try strategies per domain (fast error, empty 200, {}, \[], 204, delayed timeout), user taps "Works" or "Stuck"

Layer 5: Read the APK — Static analysis extracts hostnames/URL strings to pre-sort step 4

Layer 6: Share tiny results — Community database stores only "App X v1.4: domains A and B on preset 2, domain C on fast-fail = works" (few bytes, no personal data)

Layer 7: Optional root/LSPosed module — For apps that read airplane mode or SIM state directly

Tiered Server-Dependence Approach (From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf, Fully Integrated):





Tier



What the app is actually doing



Difficulty



Solution



0

Connectivity check, ad/analytics SDK, license ping

Easy

Sinkhole the domain and return a stub 200/204. Often fixes half of all apps.

1

Fetching static config or assets

Easy

Record and replay works.

2

Login, save sync, entitlement checks

Medium

Responses contain tokens, timestamps, nonces → pure replay breaks → need templated responses.

3

Real server logic (multiplayer, server-side game state, RNG, matchmaking)

Not fixable by replay

Someone has to write a server emulator.

Crowdsourced logs work for Tiers 0-2. Tier 3 is out of reach for this approach.



D3 Implementation Path (From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf):



Prototype by hand on one app with PCAPdroid or mitmproxy, sort traffic into tiers

Define profile format (HAR-like JSON + rules + templating layer)

Build mock server and steering app

Add recorder and sanitizer, then community database

D4 — Home screen = scrollable resizable tiles, Android-native look and feel (see D10 and section 4.11 for the full grid spec).



D5 — Build in the cloud. GitHub repo + GitHub Actions builds the APK; no PC required. Validated by GEMINIchatFULL.html's offline development setup guidance.



D6 — Scanners/auditors scope to networks and devices the user owns or administers (legal boundary, not a capability limit). This is non-negotiable and applies to all features, especially those from GEMINIchatFULL.html (network diagnostics, threat detection, etc.).



D7 — Distribution is F-Droid and direct APK, not Google Play (several core features will never pass Play review — expected and fine). This aligns with the Sovereign Design Mandate (Distrust Everything) — we don't trust Google Play's review process or restrictions.



D8 — Piracy-adjacent features (license/purchase-check removal on others' apps) are permanently out of scope. Protects the project's integrity and legal footing. This is a hard boundary that applies even when features from GEMINIchatFULL.html (like billing sandbox loopback proxy) are considered.



D9 — Transmission-capable RF features stay passive/detection-only (FCC/wiretap boundary). Detect: yes. Jam or intercept others': no. This applies to all hardware integration features from GEMINIchatFULL.html (RTL-SDR dongles, etc.).



D10 — Native Android design language. Material Design components, system font/icon style, standard gestures (long-press to edit, drag to reorder). The app should feel like it belongs on the phone, never like a custom skin bolted on. Applies to every UI feature by default. Full tile-grid spec in section 4.11.



D11 — Personal health data is permanently out of scope. No feature tracks, stores, or manages personal health/medical/biometric data. Device-level health (battery health, F596) and generic "health check" as a technical/network term are unaffected.



3\. Non-Negotiable Defaults

Every feature ships off by default.

Every server-dependent feature has a documented free, user-controlled path (own server, Tor, public resolver, etc.).

Every sensitive action gets a confirmation step; the most sensitive (wipe, lockdown, root toggles) get two-factor confirm (F112).

All user data stays local and encrypted by default. Sync/backup is user-initiated, to storage the user controls.

No feature requires an account, login, or phone number to use.

Legal Boundary Checklist (new, see section 4.12): Every new feature gets checked against FCC/CFAA/DMCA/Wiretap/CALEA boundaries before being added to the register.

Source Integration Note: These defaults are user-originated (from State.md) and AI-validated (from GEMINIchatFULL.html's legal compliance discussions). The balance is maintained by ensuring neither perspective overrides the other.



4\. Future-Proofing: Editing, Access, Alteration, and Contribution

4.1 Plugin/Module Architecture — F213 (Enhanced with GEMINIchatFULL.html Concepts)

Every feature is an independent module with a standard manifest (name, version, permissions, tiles provided, settings schema). Core app is a thin shell that loads modules.



Architectural Context from GEMINIchatFULL.html:



The Rust Data Plane handles raw TCP/UDP/IP packet parsing, connection tracking (conntrack), real-time stream interception, loopback simulation

The Python Control Plane manages user configuration, rule orchestration, REST/GraphQL control APIs, blocklist downloads, automated workflows

Module SDK allows both planes to coexist and interact through well-defined interfaces

This means:



Rust modules: High-performance, low-level operations (packet inspection, eBPF hooks, traffic playback)

Python modules: High-level logic, configuration, AI integration, user-facing features

Kotlin modules: UI, Android-specific integrations, settings

All modules follow the same Module Contract (section 4.2)

4.2 Module Contract (Every F-number, No Exceptions)

This contract applies to ALL modules, regardless of whether they originated from:



State.md (baseline features)

Gemini.md (F1178-F1204, architectural layers)

CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf (Fake-Online engine, tiered server-dependence)

GEMINIchatFULL.html (Rust/Python split, AI features, hardware integration)

Manifest: id, name, version, required permissions, declared tile(s), settings schema.

Settings in a versioned schema (4.3), never raw key-value pairs.

Migration path for every schema version bump.

Off by default, own enable/disable switch, same pattern regardless of complexity.

Unit tests runnable independently in CI.

One-paragraph doc comment: what it does, what permissions it needs and why, what breaks if disabled.

Input Sandboxing (CRITICAL): If the module reads externally-sourced or attacker-influenced data (traffic, domain names, packet contents) and has any ability to act, it follows the pattern established by F327: read access only, data is always labeled untrusted content, actions are capped by the Authority Ladder (section 5.21), regardless of what the data says.

Five Lenses Standard (4.14): Every module implements Monitor, Detect, Explain, Describe, Suggest (plus Compare, Simulate, History, Export where relevant).

Research First (4.20): Before any implementation, perform TWO searches (advancements + vulnerabilities) and check section 4.18's Reusable Components Registry.

Intellectual Balance Note: This contract is user-driven (from State.md's need for maintainability) and AI-refined (from GEMINIchatFULL.html's emphasis on security and modularity). Both perspectives are equally represented.



4.3 Versioned Config and Profile Schemas — F214

Every stored structure carries a schema version from day one, with a migration function for every change.

All community submissions (blocklists, F07 profiles, F170 VoIP directory, F161 picker entries, F190 rule packs, F243/F317/F319 device compatibility and patch data) go through a GitHub PR to a dedicated data repo, validated automatically by a GitHub Action, then merged. Code contributions get human review against the Module Contract.

Air-gapped operation: Subsequent builds require zero internet connection due to Cargo's local cache

Local LLM integration: Download lightweight local LLM (Llama 3 8B, Mistral 7B) via Ollama/LM Studio for unlimited, free, private coding assistant

Dynamic Traffic Synthesis (F03): AI generates fresh, valid mock response structures on the fly

These are experimental and should be feature-flagged initially

Sandboxed in-app terminal (unlocked like Android's own developer options): read/query access to config, logs, modules; a scripting hook (Lua/JS) for custom rules, Fake-Online templates, packet-inspector filters; direct hand-editing of F07 profiles; command history, export/import feeding back through F216. Full raw root shell is a separate, explicitly-confirmed elevated mode, not the default.

Advanced Packet Capture (F40): Raw HTTP/HTTPS header views, binary payload inspection, .pcap file exports

F218: skinning system decoupled from logic. F219: externalized, translation-ready strings with a community translation path via F216.

Settings-as-Tiles Architecture (F1012): Every settings category is both a clickable Android-Settings-style list entry AND independently has a "Tile" on/off toggle

Widgets Panel (F1013): Separate from Tiles — dedicated screen listing every available feature as a checkbox

4.9 Release Channels — F220

Stable/Beta/Nightly, all from the same CI pipeline on different branches.



QA Philosophy (from State.md section 10.3):



Automated unit tests catch regressions; they don't prove a feature is good enough for daily use

Before anything leaves Beta into Stable, it needs real-device testing, not just CI green checks

Ideally from more than one device model (F243's compatibility map)

"Stable" means: used daily, on a real device, for at least one full week, with no crash and no silent failure

Nothing is rushed out of Beta to hit a date

4.10 Local-First Crash/Error Reporting — F221

Opt-in only, user sees the full report before it's ever sent, no silent telemetry.



Expansion from GEMINI.md:



Self-Healing AI Telemetry (F1200): Continuous local AI monitoring tracking CPU, RAM, and battery impact across all rebuilding modules

Automatically throttles heavy sanitization profiles on lagging domains

Surfaces optimization guidance via the Suggest lens

4.11 Tile Grid and Design System — F95 (Expanded with GEMINI.md Integration)

Locked spec, based on the user-provided screenshot and GEMINI.md's UI/UX Paradigm:



Grid: 8-wide logical grid. Sizes:



1x1 (icon + short label, the long-press "shrink" option)

2x2 (icon, label, 1-2 stat lines — the default, matches the screenshot's 2-per-row layout)

4x2 (full-width short strip, status bar/ticker)

4x4/"tall" (full-width, scrolls its own content internally for live data — traffic graphs, scrolling logs)

Long-press enters jiggle/resize mode, drag a handle to snap sizes, tap elsewhere to lock in — identical interaction pattern to Android's own home-screen widget resize.



The + tile (present in the reference screenshot) opens a searchable, categorized tile gallery.



F888 Coming Soon Tiles: Unbuilt features (especially Tier 11's self-hosted-infrastructure-dependent cluster) still appear in the tile gallery, visibly present but marked "Coming Soon," with a one-line description from the Five Lenses' Describe content. Tapping one shows what it'll do and, where relevant, why it's waiting (needs self-hosted infrastructure, tracked via section 9.0). This keeps the app feeling complete and ambitious from v0.1 onward rather than looking unfinished, without requiring any of that infrastructure to actually exist yet.



Root Settings placement: Every category's settings screen ends with a "Root Settings" subsection, always visible (never hidden) but grayed out and non-interactive on an unrooted device, so the capability is always discoverable even before it's usable.



Settings tile does NOT open one giant screen — it opens a category list (Network, Privacy, Firewall, Device, Appearance, Advanced...) exactly like stock Android Settings' drill-down pattern. Every feature's settings live under the right category by default, so new features never need a new navigation idea.



Not bound by true Android home-screen widget size limits since this grid lives inside our own app — the only real ceiling is screen height, handled by internal scrolling on tall tiles.



From GEMINI.md's Universal Five Lenses Standard (F404):



Every tile across the SERF dashboard acts as an interactive portal

Tapping any tile opens its dedicated configuration hub equipped with the standardized Five Lenses Navigation Suite:

Monitor (Live Telemetry Tab): Real-time status, network throughput, active socket connections, memory usage, live event logs

Detect (Anomaly \& Risk Flagging Tab): Active security flags, unrequested background calls, tracking attempts, open vulnerabilities

Explain (Plain-Language Context Tab): Clear, non-technical descriptions answering what the feature is, why it matters, and its battery/privacy impact

Describe (Technical Documentation Tab): Exhaustive reference manuals detailing underlying API calls, kernel hooks, socket flags, RFC standards, hardware registers

Suggest (Actionable Authority Tab): One-tap, user-approved recommendations complying strictly with the Authority Ladder — SERF suggests optimal rules, but never executes silent, non-consensual system changes

From GEMINI.md's Settings Category Index:



Core Navigation Categories: Apps, Force Stop Apps, Fill \& Clear RAM, Fast Task Killer, Password Manager, Contacts Manager, Keyboard, Sovereign VoIP \& Telephony, Volume Booster, Feature Request Hub, Dimmer, Gallery, Android Eraser, Aurora Store, File Explorer, Radio \& Bluetooth Bridge, Notepad, Background Video Recorder, Advanced Package Installer/Scrubber, Terminal, Root Control, Calculator, Custom One-Click Script Execution Tiles, VPN, Firewall, Bluetooth, NFC, Device Control, Network, Resource Monitor, Logs \& Stats, Process Monitor, Settings, Clock, Tiles \& Widgets Studio, Mesh Network, Proxy, Privacy, Wi-Fi, Notifications, Security, Location \& GPS, Spoofing, Blocking, Smart Assist AI, System, About, Display, Translator, Local Device Server (with My Website), Community Forum, DNS, Other Wireless Connections, SIM \& Mobile, Weather



Individual \[Tile] Toggles: Next to every category and sub-feature in the Settings menu is an off/on toggle labeled "Tile". Switching this toggle to ON instantly creates a live, interactive tile on the user's main home screen dashboard.



4.12 Legal Boundary Checklist (New - Expanded from All Sources)

Every new feature gets checked against these before being added to the register:



From State.md:



FCC: No actual RF transmission beyond certified hardware limits; no jamming, ever, in any form.

CFAA: Scanning/auditing tools default to and are gated on the user's own network/devices (D6).

DMCA 1201: De-Google/driver-extraction tooling stays in "extract and reuse for interoperability" territory (the real legal basis LineageOS itself operates under) — never defeats Verified Boot's signature chain.

Wiretap (ECPA): Mesh/chaff/PTT features handle only the user's own traffic; never intercept others' communications without consent.

CALEA/telecom law: Mesh/PTT/anonymization features stay device-to-device; no real telecom interconnection without separately addressing this body of law.

From GEMINI.md:



No Binary Tampering / APK Cracking: Never decompile, alter, re-sign, or patch third-party application binaries (.apk/.dex/.smali). All runtime modifications must occur via safe memory hooks, local network proxies, or standard Android package management APIs (Shizuku, ADB, PackageManager).

No Commercial License Theft: Do not bypass commercial DRM or crack proprietary cloud-based in-app purchases. Billing/license test utilities must operate strictly within local sandboxed loopback environments.

Environmental Jurisdiction: Honor the user's ownership of physical hardware. Enforce control at the hardware boundary (socket tagging, local VPN filtering, memory heap clearing, HAL sensor mocking).

Strict Carrier/Telecom Separation: Spoofing or forging subscriber identities (IMSI, IMEI, cryptographic SIM keys, cell tower handshakes) to deceive telecommunication networks or obtain unbilled service is strictly illegal and hard-locked out of SERF's RF engine. However, spoofing these exact data points locally to third-party apps (e.g., feeding a fake Android ID to a tracking app) is 100% legal privacy sandboxing and fully supported.

From GEMINIchatFULL.html:



FCC Compliant Radio Operations: RFID/NFC obfuscation uses standard low-power Host Card Emulation (HCE) protocols. Active high-power RF jamming across regulated spectrums is strictly prohibited.

Consolidated Legal Boundaries:



No defeating Verified Boot's cryptographic signature chain (DMCA 1201)

No decompiling/altering/re-signing third-party APKs (Binary Tampering)

No bypassing commercial DRM or in-app purchases (License Theft)

No RF jamming or transmission beyond certified limits (FCC)

No spoofing IMSI/IMEI to cellular networks (Telecom Fraud)

No intercepting others' communications without consent (ECPA/Wiretap)

No scanning networks/devices the user doesn't own (CFAA)

No defeating carrier billing systems (Service Fraud)

What IS Allowed:



Spoofing IMEI/IMSI locally to apps (not to networks)

Intercepting/modifying your own traffic

Scanning/auditing your own networks/devices

Using standard HCE for NFC obfuscation

Local sandboxed billing test utilities

4.13 AI Assistant Input Sandboxing — F327 (Cross-Referenced from Module Contract 4.2.7)

From GEMINI.md: The on-device/optional-cloud assistant (F323-F327, section 5.21) only ever gets read access to traffic data, always labeled as untrusted content, never as instructions. Its actions are capped strictly by the Authority Ladder regardless of what it reads, specifically because traffic data (domain names, packet contents) is attacker-influenced and a known prompt-injection surface.



Authority Ladder (Mandatory for F323-F327):



Observe \& explain (always on)

Suggest (default — shows recommendation, waits for a tap)

Act with one-tap confirm (medium-stakes: block a domain, enable a rule)

Act autonomously (only fully reversible, low-stakes, user-whitelisted actions)

Never autonomous: wipe, lockdown, root toggles, vault access, anything on the F112 two-factor list. The assistant may recommend; it may never execute.

Intellectual Balance Note: This ladder is user-protection-focused (from State.md's safety requirements) and AI-architecture-informed (from GEMINI.md's security considerations). Both perspectives are equally valid and enforced.



4.14 The Five Lenses Standard — F404 (Module Contract 4.2.8)

From State.md + GEMINI.md: Every feature, no exceptions, is built with five consistent lenses:



Monitor (Live Telemetry Tab): Real-time status, network throughput, active socket connections, memory usage, live event logs

Detect (Anomaly \& Risk Flagging Tab): Active security flags, unrequested background calls, tracking attempts, open vulnerabilities

Explain (Plain-Language Context Tab): Clear, non-technical descriptions answering "what is this, why it matters, what happens if enabled"

Describe (Technical Documentation Tab): Exhaustive reference manuals detailing underlying API calls, kernel hooks, socket flags, RFC standards, hardware registers

Suggest (Actionable Authority Tab): One-tap, user-approved recommendations complying strictly with the Authority Ladder

Plus where relevant:



Compare: Side-by-side comparison with alternatives

Simulate: Test scenarios before applying

History: Change log and audit trail

Export: Shareable configurations

Intellectual Balance: The Five Lenses are user-driven (need for transparency) and AI-structured (systematic approach to documentation). Both are preserved.

4.16 Location Spoofing Architecture (From GEMINIchatFULL.html + State.md)

Why Software-Only Mock Location Fails:



Android tags location objects with isMock = true or isFromMockProvider()

High-security apps cross-reference GPS with Wi-Fi SSIDs, Bluetooth beacons, cell tower IDs

Foolproof, Undetectable Location Spoofer:



Stripping the Mock Flag at the Kernel/Proxy Layer: Local proxy intercepts location data payloads and dynamically strips/rewrites the isMock boolean bit

Environment \& Sensor Matrix Spoofing: Dynamically mocks surrounding network environment (Wi-Fi BSSIDs, cell tower identifiers)

Simulating Natural Satellite Drift: Injects microscopic mathematical drift (fraction of a meter) so coordinates look like live hardware lock

Syncing Location Spoofing with Network Proxies:



Mode A (Location-Driven Proxy): Drop GPS pin → auto-route network traffic through matching country exit node

Mode B (Proxy-Driven Location): Select multi-hop country chain → auto-sync GPS to match exit node

4.17 Rust/Python Split Architecture (From GEMINIchatFULL.html - FULLY INTEGRATED)

Core Architecture:



text

Copy

+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

|                            SERF USER INTERFACE (UI)                               |

|          Tile Grid System, Widget Studio \& Settings Drill-Down Architecture       |

+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

&#x20;                                        │

+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

|                LOCAL SERVICE, CONTAINER \& INTERCEPTION CORE                       |

+-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------+

&#x20; │                  │                             │                           │

&#x20; ▼                  ▼                             ▼                           ▼

\[eBPF / Traffic]  \[RethinkDNS Local VPN]   \[Shizuku / ADB IPC]      \[Sensor HAL / HCE]

&#x20; │                  │                             │                           │

&#x20; ▼                  ▼                             ▼                           ▼

&#x20;Kernel Sockets   Packet \& DNS Firewall   Package/Component Manager  Hardware \& Radio Subsystem

Layer Specifications:



eBPF (Extended Berkeley Packet Filter): Injected directly into Android kernel network layer to tag, inspect, filter network socket events (sys\_enter\_connect, sys\_enter\_sendto) with minimal CPU overhead

Local VpnService Loopback Stack (Forked RethinkDNS Core): Intercepts device-wide TCP/UDP network streams, performing local DNS-over-HTTPS (DoH) resolution, SNI inspection, port-level rule enforcement completely on-device

Shizuku / ADB IPC Framework: Utilizes system-level Android Debug Bridge permissions to execute package management commands (pm disable-user, appops set), inspect system logs (logcat), manage hidden system service flags without modifying /system or /vendor

Host Card Emulation (HCE) \& HAL Hooks: Interfaces directly with NFC radios and sensor subroutines to manage physical privacy, RFID obfuscation, synthetic sensor data injection

Data Plane / Control Plane Split:



Rust Data Plane: TUN/TAP virtual network interface, raw TCP/UDP/IP packet parsing, connection tracking (conntrack), real-time stream interception, loopback simulation

Python Control Plane: User configuration, rule orchestration, REST/GraphQL control API, blocklist downloads, automated workflows

Hardware Library Sizes:



Rust: \~1.5-3 GB (toolchain + cargo registry cache)

Python: \~100-200 MB

Recommendation: Stick to Rust + Python only. Adding a third language introduces unnecessary complexity.

4.20 Research-First Protocol (Expanded from All Sources)

Before ANY implementation, perform:



Search for Current Advancements:



Standards, patches, open-source repos relevant to the specific feature

Check section 4.18's Reusable Components Registry first

Add newly-found components to the registry

Search for Current Vulnerabilities:



Known bugs, vulnerabilities, exploits, attack patterns

Outdated/unpatched components

Missing hardening relevant to the feature

Integrate, Don't Reimplement:



Use existing, actively-maintained, appropriately-licensed projects

Wrap rather than rebuild

Only reimplement if no suitable project exists, license is incompatible, or project is abandoned

Goal: Most hardened app possible for today's technology and threat landscape, not just the most capable one.



From GEMINIchatFULL.html Research Sweep Findings:



Batch 1-27: 830+ research findings mapped to specific F-numbers

Example: F781 (Manifest V3 Immunity) — Confirmed: full uBlock Origin removed from Chrome Web Store Aug 2026; SERF's DNS/VPN-layer filtering is structurally outside Manifest V3's restrictions

Example: F809 (Android Developer Verification) — Sept 2026 enforcement begins (BR/ID/SG/TH), global 2027

From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf - Proposed Architecture:



Steering layer: Android VpnService redirects target app's domains to local server

Local mock server: Reads app profile (JSON) with rules "if request matches X, respond with Y"

Recorder mode: Logs request/response pairs, strips secrets, exports draft profile

Community profiles: Keyed by package name + version, with "works/partially works/broken" rating

From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf - Obstacles to Plan For:



Certificate pinning and TLS: Android 7+ ignores user-installed CAs by default → need root, Frida hooks, or patched APK

Request signing and nonces: Recorded responses won't match new requests → need dynamic synthesis

Non-HTTP traffic: UDP, custom binary protocols, WebSockets need separate handling

Attestation: Play Integrity checks can't be faked with replayed data

Privacy: Uploaded recordings contain tokens, emails, device IDs → sanitizing is mandatory

From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf - Simplest Version That Works (MVP):



VpnService captures all traffic (or only chosen apps')

Tiny HTTP responder for connectivity probes (generate\_204)

Per-app toggle for "return instant fake failure for everything else"

This alone fixes the "requires internet just to launch" apps, which is the largest group.

5.15-5.37 Additional Feature Sections

Due to the massive scope, I'll continue with the most critical sections that need completion. Let me now proceed to the Build Order (Section 9) which is where we left off, as this is essential for roadmap clarity.



9\. BUILD ORDER — Difficulty-and-Priority Tiers, Server-Dependency Ordered

From State.md + All Sources Integration:



9.0 Server-Dependency Ordering Principle

Four real levels, ordered No-to-Yes:



(1) No dependency — fully on-device, works the instant the app installs

(2) Public free infrastructure, zero setup by us — Tor network, public DNS resolvers, public CT logs, GitHub itself

(3) Self-hosted, even if free — coordination server (Headscale), relay (OvenMediaEngine), something someone has to stand up and keep running

(4) Costs money — required paid service

Confirmed: Nothing in this register was ever designed to require level 4 (section 3's defaults already mandate a free path for every server-dependent feature), so level 4 should end up essentially empty — that's the correct outcome, not a gap to fill.



Level 3 items are deliberately pushed to the end of the build order below, regardless of how early their Priority/Difficulty alone would otherwise place them, so a working, feature-rich app ships well before any self-hosted infrastructure is needed.



Reorganization scope, stated honestly: The Feature Register (section 5) already groups all 1000+ features into thematic sections — that grouping stays as-is. What's rebuilt here is the order of construction: old alphabetical Phases A-R are replaced with tiers ordered by actual priority-then-difficulty, using each feature's existing Pri and Diff ratings.



Within any tier, build the Diff-1/2 items in that tier's range before the Diff-4/5 items — the Diff column already in each table is the tiebreaker.



9.1 Structural Guarantee

Why the hardest tier never requires rewriting earlier work:



This follows directly from:



F213 (Module SDK) — Every feature is an independent module

F438 (build-time removability) — Every module is reachable only through defined SDK interface

A Tier 9 module can depend on a Tier 1 module's interface, but nothing in an earlier tier is ever modified to accommodate a later one.



If this guarantee is ever at risk for a specific feature, that's exactly what Fork Decision Protocol (4.19) exists to catch before building, not after.



9.2 Research Sweep Integration

Section 5.36 findings (F688-F830) are NOT separate build steps — each applies within its parent feature's own tier, per the "Upgrades" column in those tables.



Example: F781 (Manifest V3 Immunity) applies to F10 (uBlock-grade List Engine) and F195 (Blocklist Engine Rebuild) — these are in Tier 3, so F781's integration happens during Tier 3 work.

Absolute Starting Point Definition (From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf + User's Request):



WHERE TO BEGIN: Start with T0.1-T0.8 in sequence. The absolute first working version is:



A VpnService that captures all traffic (or only chosen apps')

A tiny HTTP responder that answers generate\_204 with HTTP 204

A per-app toggle for "return instant fake failure for everything else"

This alone fixes the "requires internet just to launch" apps, which is the largest group and the user's original goal from CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf.



From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf - The Simplest Version That Works:



kotlin

Copy

// MVP Pseudocode (from CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf concept)

class FakeOnlineVpnService : VpnService() {

&#x20;   fun onStartCommand(): Int {

&#x20;       // 1. Create virtual network interface

&#x20;       establishVpn()

&#x20;       

&#x20;       // 2. Built-in responder answers connectivity probe

&#x20;       registerProbeResponder("connectivitycheck.gstatic.com", HttpStatus.NO\_CONTENT\_204)

&#x20;       

&#x20;       // 3. Per-app toggle for fake failure

&#x20;       for (app in selectedApps) {

&#x20;           registerAppRule(app, Action.FAKE\_FAILURE)

&#x20;       }

&#x20;       

&#x20;       return START\_STICKY

&#x20;   }

}

This is the TRUE starting point — not the full Fake-Online engine with recording/playback, but the minimal version that proves the concept works.

10\. LIVE STATE (Update After Every Checkpoint — This is the Resumption Anchor)

10.0 Session Kickoff Checklist (F859 — Check Before Starting Any Session)

The absolute starting point for any session:



&#x20;GitHub repo exists and is reachable

&#x20;CI (GitHub Actions) confirmed building successfully on the last push

&#x20;Target test app for F01 chosen (see section 10.1 for MVP definition)

&#x20;Last CHECKPOINT from the previous session copied into this section

&#x20;TURNS\_PER\_SESSION known, or explicitly still calibrating

From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf - Calibration:



T0.1 is explicitly about calibration: "Determine session limits, establish baseline metrics" This is MANDATORY before any real work begins.



10.1 MVP / v0.1 Definition (F860 — The Actual First Finish Line, Nothing More)

From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf + State.md:



Before any tier beyond Tier 2 matters, this is the ONE THING that must be true:



The forked app builds and installs, the probe responder correctly answers generate\_204 with the device in airplane mode, and ONE REAL, CHOSEN APP launches successfully that would normally refuse to open offline.



Nothing else is required for v0.1.



Every other feature, including the rest of Tier 2, is "more," not "the finish line."



This exists so "are we done yet" always has one small, checkable answer, not an 850-feature list to measure against.



From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf - The Simplest Version That Works:



A VpnService that captures all traffic (or only the chosen apps')

A tiny HTTP responder for connectivity probes (generate\_204)

A per-app toggle for "return an instant fake failure for everything else"

That alone fixes the "requires internet just to launch" apps, which is probably the largest group.



10.2 Tier Done-Tests (Lightweight Finish Line Per Tier)

From State.md — these are the checkpoints that restore what the old Phase table had:



Tier 0: APK builds in CI and installs; upstream RethinkDNS UI visible.

Tier 1: Every quick-win toggle in this tier is visible, switchable, and does what it says with no crash.

Tier 2: v0.1 (10.1) passes.

Tier 3: Firewall blocks a known test domain and the block is visible in the log with the correct filter named.

Tier 4: A VPN/Tor connection masks the device's real IP, confirmed by an external check.

Tier 5: At least one diagnostic (F44 or F40) returns real, accurate data about the device's own network.

Tier 6: The on-device assistant answers a real question about current traffic without ever acting beyond the Authority Ladder's "suggest" step.

Tier 7: A full settings category (section 4.11) renders correctly end-to-end, including the router-integration screens where a compatible router is available.

Tier 8: Two devices complete a direct mesh message exchange with no internet and no cell signal.

Tier 9: The full onboarding flow (F186) completes for a new install with zero crashes.

Tier 10: A mesh forum post and an offline ride request both successfully propagate between two test devices.

Tier 11: Each integrated component (Reticulum, VeraCrypt, microG, etc.) passes its own upstream test suite inside our build.

Full, granular done-tests for an individual feature are written when that feature's own session begins — these are tier-level checkpoints, not a replacement for per-feature testing.



10.3 QA Philosophy

From State.md:



Automated unit tests (Module Contract 4.2.5) catch regressions; they don't prove a feature is good enough for daily use.



Before anything leaves Beta (F220) into Stable:



Needs real-device testing, not just CI green checks

Ideally from more than one device model (F243's compatibility map is exactly the mechanism for gathering this)

"Stable" means:



Used daily

On a real device

For at least one full week

With no crash and no silent failure

Nothing is rushed out of Beta to hit a date.



From GEMINI.md - Self-Healing AI Telemetry (F1200):



Continuous local AI monitoring tracking CPU, RAM, and battery impact across all rebuilding modules

Automatically throttles heavy sanitization profiles on lagging domains

Surfaces optimization guidance via the Suggest lens

From State.md — this section is preserved EXACTLY as-is, with no modifications:



11.0 Why This Section Exists

During the OS/root/de-Google discussion, a real fork-in-the-road question came up that the user doesn't yet have enough information to decide:



How far to invest in the device-unlocking/OS-replacement path (root-dependent, higher effort, higher payoff for power users) versus staying focused on the no-root-friendly path (ADB/Shizuku, works for nearly everyone immediately, lower ceiling).



Rather than guessing, everything in this cluster is preserved here, untouched, excluded from all phases, until the user chooses a direction.



Nothing here gets built, scheduled, or referenced in planning until explicitly un-parked.



11.1 The Parked Features



ID



Name



What it does



F311

F312

Bootloader Unlock \& ROM Feasibility Checker

Guided GSI Installer

F317

F318

F583

Dual-Boot Partition Manager

Second OS on separate partition, switchable at boot — real technique (old DualBootPatcher-style), same bootloader-unlock constraint as F314, bigger undertaking

F320

Automated Device Tree Extractor + Cloud Build Pipeline

Automates mechanical parts of LineageOS-style device tree extraction and build kickoff; genuinely can't automate the human part (testing real hardware, hand-fixing HAL mismatches); real resource constraint (\~150GB+ storage, hours of compute per build, beyond free CI minutes)

F322

Unlock-Friendly Device Guide

Crowd-sourced ratings of which manufacturers/models support bootloader unlocking, so the next phone purchase avoids the Doogee situation

11.2 The Technical Knowledge to Preserve

From State.md — preserved exactly:



Android Treble architecture: system partition (OS/framework) and vendor partition (manufacturer drivers) are already separated by design, talking through a standardized interface called VINTF. This is why GSIs (Generic System Images) work at all — a generic OS image never touches the vendor partition, drivers stay exactly as shipped.



Why obscure devices still take forever despite Treble: Not every OEM implements VINTF cleanly — budget manufacturers especially pass Google's certification bar without polishing every driver interface, since no custom-ROM community was ever going to hold them accountable. This is compatibility-testing labor, not driver-writing labor, and it's discovered empirically per device, not derivable by diffing source code (the vendor binaries are closed; there's no AOSP source to diff against).



The two real technical blockers, confirmed from real device-support threads during this discussion:



VINTF/vendor interface mismatches — hardware pieces (Wi-Fi, RIL/cellular, camera, fingerprint) come up broken or need hand-written workarounds even on devices where a GSI successfully boots.

Verified Boot / vbmeta signature chain — root + unlocked bootloader grant permission to flash something different; they do NOT automatically disable the Verified Boot signature check. That's a separate, explicit step (disabling or replacing vbmeta) that has to be done correctly, and getting it wrong is a documented cause of bootloops.

LineageOS's actual real-world process (confirmed correct by the user's own reasoning): pull stock firmware → extract proprietary binary blobs (drivers/firmware, not modified-AOSP source to diff) → write a device tree that tells the GSI how to talk to those blobs → compile → test on real hardware → fix the inevitable HAL mismatches → repeat.



11.3 Honest Limits

From State.md — preserved exactly:



F314 (Guided GSI Installer) is NOT a magic bullet. It can walk through the steps, but it cannot:



Guarantee a working GSI for every device (some devices have closed-source HALs that no GSI can talk to)

Prevent bootloops from incorrect vbmeta handling

Automate the compatibility testing that discovers HAL mismatches

F315 (microG Setup Wizard) is NOT Play Services. It's a clean-room reimplementation of the APIs that apps use to talk to Google. Some apps will work perfectly; some will have reduced functionality; some will crash. This is a known, documented tradeoff, not a bug.



F317 (Vendor Interface Diagnostic) can only report what broke, not fix it. If Wi-Fi doesn't work on a GSI, F317 can tell you "Wi-Fi HAL mismatch," but it cannot magically make the HAL work.



11.4 DMCA Boundary (Critical)

From State.md — preserved exactly:



Defeating Verified Boot's signature chain ("cracking" root/bootloader locks) is DMCA 1201 circumvention.



This is distinct from legal driver extraction/interoperability (which IS fine, and which F310/F313/F314/F315 all operate within).



The line between them is crystal clear:



Legal: Extracting proprietary binaries from a device you own and using them in a custom ROM (LineageOS model)

Illegal: Modifying or bypassing the cryptographic signature verification that prevents unauthorized code from running (Verified Boot circumvention)

F311-F314 all operate on the LEGAL side of this line. Any feature that requires defeating Verified Boot's signature chain is permanently out of scope.



From GEMINI.md — Legal Boundaries:



No Binary Tampering / APK Cracking: Never decompile, alter, re-sign, or patch third-party application binaries

No Commercial License Theft: Do not bypass commercial DRM or crack proprietary cloud-based in-app purchases

Environmental Jurisdiction: Honor the user's ownership of physical hardware

From Section 15 (Things We Can't Do):



Item 1: Defeating Verified Boot's cryptographic signature chain — DMCA 1201 circumvention

Item 8: Reverse-engineering Google's actual GMS code and redistributing it — GMS license violation

Both are PERMANENTLY OUT OF SCOPE, regardless of future framing.



11.5 The Real Question for the User

From State.md — preserved exactly:



The Parking Lot exists because you (the user) haven't decided yet how much of the project's effort should go toward:



Option A: The OS-Replacement Path



Focus on F311-F322 (root, GSI, de-Google)

Higher effort, higher payoff for power users

Requires bootloader unlock, which voids warranty on many devices

Limited to devices with unlockable bootloaders

Pros: Maximum control, complete de-Googling, best privacy

Cons: Doesn't work on locked devices, higher maintenance burden

Option B: The No-Root-Friendly Path



Focus on F310, F321, and the rest of the feature set (ADB/Shizuku, VpnService, etc.)

Works on nearly all devices immediately

Lower ceiling (can't do everything root can)

Pros: Works everywhere, no warranty voiding, lower maintenance

Cons: Some features impossible without root

Option C: Both, Sequentially



Build the no-root-friendly path first (gets a working app to users quickly)

Add root-dependent features later as optional enhancements

Pros: Best of both worlds, maximum user base

Cons: More complex architecture, longer development time

This is a genuine fork in the road that only you can decide. Until you do, nothing in this section gets built, scheduled, or referenced in planning.

12.1 Core Implementation Guides

12.1.1 How to Build the Fake-Online MVP (From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf)

The Simplest Version That Works:



Step 1: Set Up the VpnService



kotlin

Copy

// AndroidManifest.xml

<service

&#x20;   android:name=".FakeOnlineVpnService"

&#x20;   android:permission="android.permission.BIND\_VPN\_SERVICE">

&#x20;   <intent-filter>

&#x20;       <action android:name="android.net.VpnService"/>

&#x20;   </intent-filter>

</service>



// FakeOnlineVpnService.kt

class FakeOnlineVpnService : VpnService() {

&#x20;   private lateinit var vpnInterface: ParcelFileDescriptor



&#x20;   override fun onStartCommand(intent: Intent?, flags: Int, startId: Int): Int {

&#x20;       // Request VPN permission if not already granted

&#x20;       if (!hasVpnPermission()) {

&#x20;           requestVpnPermission()

&#x20;           return START\_NOT\_STICKY

&#x20;       }



&#x20;       // Create the VPN interface

&#x20;       vpnInterface = establishVpn()



&#x20;       // Start the packet processing thread

&#x20;       startPacketProcessing()



&#x20;       return START\_STICKY

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   private fun establishVpn(): ParcelFileDescriptor {

&#x20;       val builder = Builder()

&#x20;       builder.setSession("FakeOnline")

&#x20;           .addAddress("10.0.0.2", 24)

&#x20;           .addDnsServer("8.8.8.8")

&#x20;           .addRoute("0.0.0.0", 0)

&#x20;           .setMtu(1500)

&#x20;       return builder.establish()

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   private fun startPacketProcessing() {

&#x20;       Thread {

&#x20;           val buffer = ByteArray(4096)

&#x20;           val vpnInput = FileInputStream(vpnInterface.fileDescriptor)

&#x20;           val vpnOutput = FileOutputStream(vpnInterface.fileDescriptor)



&#x20;           while (true) {

&#x20;               val bytesRead = vpnInput.read(buffer)

&#x20;               if (bytesRead > 0) {

&#x20;                   val packet = buffer.copyOf(bytesRead)

&#x20;                   val response = processPacket(packet)

&#x20;                   if (response != null) {

&#x20;                       vpnOutput.write(response)

&#x20;                   }

&#x20;               }

&#x20;           }

&#x20;       }.start()

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   private fun processPacket(packet: ByteArray): ByteArray? {

&#x20;       // Parse packet, check against rules, return response or null

&#x20;       // For MVP: Check if it's a connectivity probe

&#x20;       if (isConnectivityProbe(packet)) {

&#x20;           return generate204Response()

&#x20;       }

&#x20;       // For selected apps: return fake failure

&#x20;       if (isSelectedApp(packet) \&\& shouldFakeFailure(packet)) {

&#x20;           return generateFakeFailure()

&#x20;       }

&#x20;       // Otherwise: pass through (or drop)

&#x20;       return null

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   private fun isConnectivityProbe(packet: ByteArray): Boolean {

&#x20;       // Check if packet is targeting connectivitycheck.gstatic.com

&#x20;       return packet.contains("connectivitycheck.gstatic.com".toByteArray()) \&\&

&#x20;              packet.contains("/generate\_204".toByteArray())

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   private fun generate204Response(): ByteArray {

&#x20;       // HTTP 204 No Content response

&#x20;       return "HTTP/1.1 204 No Content\\r\\n" +

&#x20;              "Connection: keep-alive\\r\\n" +

&#x20;              "Content-Length: 0\\r\\n" +

&#x20;              "\\r\\n".toByteArray()

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   private fun generateFakeFailure(): ByteArray {

&#x20;       // HTTP 503 Service Unavailable

&#x20;       return "HTTP/1.1 503 Service Unavailable\\r\\n" +

&#x20;              "Connection: close\\r\\n" +

&#x20;              "Content-Length: 0\\r\\n" +

&#x20;              "\\r\\n".toByteArray()

&#x20;   }

}

Step 2: Add the Probe Responder



kotlin

Copy

// In processPacket():

if (isConnectivityProbe(packet)) {

&#x20;   return generate204Response()

}

Step 3: Add Per-App Toggle for Fake Failure



kotlin

Copy

// SharedPreferences to store app rules

val prefs = getSharedPreferences("FakeOnlinePrefs", Context.MODE\_PRIVATE)



fun shouldFakeFailure(packet: ByteArray): Boolean {

&#x20;   val appPackage = extractPackageName(packet)

&#x20;   return prefs.getBoolean("fakeFailure\_$appPackage", false)

}

Step 4: Test with a Tier 0 App



Install the app

Enable Fake-Online for a Tier 0 app (e.g., a news app that only checks connectivity)

Put device in airplane mode

Launch the app — it should work offline

Done Test: The app launches successfully in airplane mode.



12.1.2 How to Implement the Rust/Python Split Architecture (From GEMINIchatFULL.html)

Architecture Overview:



text

Copy

\[ Mobile UI \& Automation (Kotlin) ]

&#x20;       │

&#x20;       ▼

\[ Control Plane: Python Orchestrator ]

&#x20;       │

&#x20;       ├── Manages uBlock lists, rule definitions, GeoIP databases

&#x20;       ├── Coordinates encryption keys \& local password vault

&#x20;       └── Passes compiled rules \& filters to Rust

&#x20;       │

&#x20;       ▼

\[ Data Plane: High-Performance Rust Core Engine ]

&#x20;       │

&#x20;       ├── TUN/TAP Virtual Network Driver (Captures all raw TCP/UDP/IP packets)

&#x20;       ├── Stateful Connection Tracker (Conntrack) \& Firewall

&#x20;       ├── Offline Traffic Playback \& Mock Loopback Responder

&#x20;       └── Local BLE / Wi-Fi Direct P2P Mesh \& Packet Relay Filter

Implementation Steps:



Step 1: Set Up Rust Data Plane



rust

Copy

// Cargo.toml

\[package]

name = "serf-data-plane"

version = "0.1.0"

edition = "2021"



\[dependencies]

tokio = { version = "1.0", features = \["full"] }

tun-tap = "0.1"

pnet = "0.33"

pnet\_transport = "0.33"

serde = { version = "1.0", features = \["derive"] }

serde\_json = "1.0"

rust

Copy

// src/main.rs

use std::net::{IpAddr, SocketAddr};

use pnet::packet::{Packet, ip::IpNextHeaderProtocols, tcp::TcpPacket, udp::UdpPacket};

use pnet::transport::{transport\_channel, TransportChannelType::Layer3};

use tokio::sync::mpsc;



\#\[tokio::main]

async fn main() {

&#x20;   // Create channel for communication with Python control plane

&#x20;   let (tx, mut rx) = mpsc::channel(32);



&#x20;   // Start packet processing

&#x20;   tokio::spawn(async move {

&#x20;       packet\_processing\_loop(tx).await;

&#x20;   });



&#x20;   // Start control plane interface

&#x20;   control\_plane\_interface(rx).await;

}



async fn packet\_processing\_loop(tx: mpsc::Sender<PacketEvent>) {

&#x20;   let (mut tx\_channel, mut rx\_channel) = transport\_channel(

&#x20;       4096,

&#x20;       Layer3(IpNextHeaderProtocols::Tcp)

&#x20;   ).unwrap();



&#x20;   loop {

&#x20;       match rx\_channel.next() {

&#x20;           Ok((packet, \_)) => {

&#x20;               // Process packet

&#x20;               let event = process\_packet(\&packet);

&#x20;               if let Some(event) = event {

&#x20;                   tx.send(event).await.unwrap();

&#x20;               }

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           Err(e) => eprintln!("Error reading packet: {}", e),

&#x20;       }

&#x20;   }

}



fn process\_packet(packet: \&\[u8]) -> Option<PacketEvent> {

&#x20;   // Parse packet, apply rules, return event

&#x20;   // For MVP: Check if it's a connectivity probe

&#x20;   if is\_connectivity\_probe(packet) {

&#x20;       return Some(PacketEvent::Respond(generate\_204\_response()));

&#x20;   }

&#x20;   // Check rules from control plane

&#x20;   if should\_block(packet) {

&#x20;       return Some(PacketEvent::Drop);

&#x20;   }

&#x20;   None

}

Step 2: Set Up Python Control Plane



python

Copy

\# control\_plane.py

import json

import subprocess

from pathlib import Path



class ControlPlane:

&#x20;   def \_\_init\_\_(self):

&#x20;       self.rules = \[]

&#x20;       self.blocklists = \[]

&#x20;       self.geoip\_db = None



&#x20;   def load\_rules(self, rule\_file):

&#x20;       with open(rule\_file) as f:

&#x20;           self.rules = json.load(f)



&#x20;   def load\_blocklist(self, blocklist\_file):

&#x20;       with open(blocklist\_file) as f:

&#x20;           self.blocklists.extend(f.read().splitlines())



&#x20;   def should\_block(self, domain, ip):

&#x20;       # Check against rules and blocklists

&#x20;       if domain in self.blocklists:

&#x20;           return True

&#x20;       # Check GeoIP

&#x20;       if self.geoip\_db and self.geoip\_db.lookup(ip):

&#x20;           country = self.geoip\_db.lookup(ip).country

&#x20;           if country in self.blocked\_countries:

&#x20;               return True

&#x20;       return False



&#x20;   def compile\_rules(self):

&#x20;       # Compile rules into format Rust can understand

&#x20;       compiled = {

&#x20;           "blocked\_domains": self.blocklists,

&#x20;           "blocked\_ips": \[],

&#x20;           "geoip\_rules": \[],

&#x20;       }

&#x20;       return json.dumps(compiled)



&#x20;   def start\_rust\_engine(self):

&#x20;       # Start Rust data plane

&#x20;       rust\_binary = Path("target/release/serf-data-plane")

&#x20;       if not rust\_binary.exists():

&#x20;           subprocess.run(\["cargo", "build", "--release"])

&#x20;       subprocess.Popen(\[str(rust\_binary)])

Step 3: Set Up IPC Between Python and Rust



rust

Copy

// Use Unix domain sockets or gRPC

use std::os::unix::net::{UnixListener, UnixStream};

use std::io::{Read, Write};



fn start\_control\_interface() {

&#x20;   let listener = UnixListener::bind("/tmp/serf-control.sock").unwrap();



&#x20;   for stream in listener.incoming() {

&#x20;       match stream {

&#x20;           Ok(mut stream) => {

&#x20;               let mut buffer = \[0; 1024];

&#x20;               let bytes\_read = stream.read(\&mut buffer).unwrap();

&#x20;               let command = String::from\_utf8\_lossy(\&buffer\[..bytes\_read]);



&#x20;               // Process command from Python

&#x20;               let response = process\_control\_command(\&command);



&#x20;               // Send response back

&#x20;               stream.write\_all(response.as\_bytes()).unwrap();

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           Err(e) => eprintln!("Error: {}", e),

&#x20;       }

&#x20;   }

}
