12.1.3 How to Implement the Tiered Server-Dependence Engine (From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf)

Concept:





Tier



What the app is doing



Difficulty



Solution



0

Connectivity check, ad/analytics SDK, license ping

Easy

Sinkhole domain, return stub 200/204

1

Fetching static config or assets

Easy

Record and replay

2

Login, save sync, entitlement checks

Medium

Templated responses

3

Real server logic

Not fixable

Server emulator

Implementation:



kotlin

Copy

// TierClassifier.kt

class TierClassifier {

&#x20;   private val tier0Domains = setOf(

&#x20;       "connectivitycheck.gstatic.com",

&#x20;       "google.com",

&#x20;       "googlesyndication.com",

&#x20;       "doubleclick.net",

&#x20;       // Add more analytics/ad domains

&#x20;   )



&#x20;   private val tier1Domains = setOf(

&#x20;       // Static config domains

&#x20;   )



&#x20;   private val tier2Domains = setOf(

&#x20;       // Login/sync domains

&#x20;   )



&#x20;   fun classify(domain: String, request: HttpRequest): ServerDependencyTier {

&#x20;       if (tier0Domains.contains(domain)) {

&#x20;           return ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_0

&#x20;       }

&#x20;       if (tier1Domains.contains(domain)) {

&#x20;           return ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_1

&#x20;       }

&#x20;       if (tier2Domains.contains(domain)) {

&#x20;           return ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_2

&#x20;       }

&#x20;       return ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_3

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   fun getResponseStrategy(tier: ServerDependencyTier, request: HttpRequest): ResponseStrategy {

&#x20;       return when (tier) {

&#x20;           ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_0 -> ResponseStrategy.Stub204

&#x20;           ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_1 -> ResponseStrategy.RecordReplay

&#x20;           ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_2 -> ResponseStrategy.Templated

&#x20;           ServerDependencyTier.TIER\_3 -> ResponseStrategy.FailFast

&#x20;       }

&#x20;   }

}



enum class ServerDependencyTier {

&#x20;   TIER\_0, TIER\_1, TIER\_2, TIER\_3

}



enum class ResponseStrategy {

&#x20;   Stub204, RecordReplay, Templated, FailFast

}

Record Mode:



kotlin

Copy

// Recorder.kt

class Recorder {

&#x20;   private val recordings = mutableMapOf<String, MutableList<RequestResponsePair>>()



&#x20;   fun record(request: HttpRequest, response: HttpResponse) {

&#x20;       val key = "${request.method}:${request.domain}:${request.path}"

&#x20;       recordings.getOrPut(key) { mutableListOf() }.add(

&#x20;           RequestResponsePair(request, response)

&#x20;       )

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   fun saveProfile(appPackage: String, version: String) {

&#x20;       val profile = Profile(

&#x20;           packageName = appPackage,

&#x20;           version = version,

&#x20;           recordings = recordings.values.flatten().toList(),

&#x20;           tierClassification = classifyRecordings()

&#x20;       )

&#x20;       // Save to file or upload to community database

&#x20;   }

}

Replay Mode:



kotlin

Copy

// Replayer.kt

class Replayer {

&#x20;   private val profiles = mutableMapOf<String, Profile>()



&#x20;   fun loadProfile(appPackage: String, version: String) {

&#x20;       // Load from file or download from community database

&#x20;       profiles\[appPackage] = loadProfileFromStorage(appPackage, version)

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   fun getResponse(request: HttpRequest): HttpResponse? {

&#x20;       val profile = profiles\[request.appPackage] ?: return null

&#x20;       val key = "${request.method}:${request.domain}:${request.path}"



&#x20;       // Find matching recording

&#x20;       val recording = profile.recordings.find {

&#x20;           it.request.method == request.method \&\&

&#x20;           it.request.domain == request.domain \&\&

&#x20;           it.request.path == request.path

&#x20;       }



&#x20;       return recording?.response?.copy(

&#x20;           // Update dynamic fields (timestamps, nonces)

&#x20;           headers = updateDynamicHeaders(recording.response.headers, request)

&#x20;       )

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   private fun updateDynamicHeaders(headers: Map<String, String>, request: HttpRequest): Map<String, String> {

&#x20;       val updated = headers.toMutableMap()

&#x20;       // Replace timestamp with current time

&#x20;       updated\["Date"] = Instant.now().toHttpDate()

&#x20;       // Replace nonce with new value

&#x20;       updated\["X-Nonce"] = generateNonce()

&#x20;       return updated

&#x20;   }

}

Done Test: Tier 0-2 apps work offline with appropriate response strategies.



12.1.4 How to Implement the Five Lenses Standard (From GEMINI.md + State.md)

Every feature implements five consistent lenses:



kotlin

Copy

// FiveLenses.kt

interface FiveLensesFeature {

&#x20;   // 1. Monitor: Live Telemetry Tab

&#x20;   fun getMonitorView(context: Context): View



&#x20;   // 2. Detect: Anomaly \& Risk Flagging Tab

&#x20;   fun getDetectView(context: Context): View

&#x20;   fun getAnomalies(): List<Anomaly>



&#x20;   // 3. Explain: Plain-Language Context Tab

&#x20;   fun getExplainText(): String

&#x20;   fun getWhyItMatters(): String

&#x20;   fun getImpactDescription(): String



&#x20;   // 4. Describe: Technical Documentation Tab

&#x20;   fun getDescribeText(): String

&#x20;   fun getApiDocumentation(): String

&#x20;   fun getImplementationDetails(): String



&#x20;   // 5. Suggest: Actionable Authority Tab

&#x20;   fun getSuggestions(): List<Suggestion>

&#x20;   fun onSuggestionSelected(suggestion: Suggestion)

}



// Example implementation for Firewall feature

class FirewallFeature : FiveLensesFeature {

&#x20;   override fun getMonitorView(context: Context): View {

&#x20;       // Return live traffic view

&#x20;       return TrafficMonitorView(context)

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getDetectView(context: Context): View {

&#x20;       // Return anomaly detection view

&#x20;       return AnomalyView(context, getAnomalies())

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getAnomalies(): List<Anomaly> {

&#x20;       return listOf(

&#x20;           Anomaly(

&#x20;               type = AnomalyType.UNEXPECTED\_CONNECTION,

&#x20;               severity = Severity.HIGH,

&#x20;               description = "App X is connecting to unknown domain Y",

&#x20;               timestamp = Instant.now()

&#x20;           )

&#x20;       )

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getExplainText(): String {

&#x20;       return "The firewall blocks apps from connecting to the internet. " +

&#x20;              "This protects your privacy by preventing apps from sending your data " +

&#x20;              "to tracking companies or other third parties."

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getWhyItMatters(): String {

&#x20;       return "Many apps send your data to tracking companies without your knowledge. " +

&#x20;              "Blocking these connections gives you control over your personal information."

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getImpactDescription(): String {

&#x20;       return "Some apps may not work correctly if they require internet access. " +

&#x20;              "You can allow specific apps or domains through the firewall."

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getDescribeText(): String {

&#x20;       return "The firewall uses Android's VpnService API to intercept all network traffic. " +

&#x20;              "It matches each connection against a set of rules that you define. " +

&#x20;              "Connections that match a block rule are dropped. " +

&#x20;              "Connections that match an allow rule are permitted."

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getApiDocumentation(): String {

&#x20;       return "See: android.net.VpnService, android.net.ConnectivityManager"

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getImplementationDetails(): String {

&#x20;       return "The firewall is implemented as a VpnService that creates a virtual network interface. " +

&#x20;              "All traffic from apps is routed through this interface. " +

&#x20;              "The service inspects each packet and applies the firewall rules. " +

&#x20;              "Blocked packets are dropped. Allowed packets are forwarded to the real network."

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun getSuggestions(): List<Suggestion> {

&#x20;       return listOf(

&#x20;           Suggestion(

&#x20;               title = "Block all trackers",

&#x20;               description = "Block connections to known tracking domains",

&#x20;               action = { enableTrackerBlocking() },

&#x20;               authorityLevel = AuthorityLevel.SUGGEST

&#x20;           ),

&#x20;           Suggestion(

&#x20;               title = "Allow this app",

&#x20;               description = "Allow App X to connect to the internet",

&#x20;               action = { allowApp("com.example.app") },

&#x20;               authorityLevel = AuthorityLevel.ACT\_WITH\_CONFIRM

&#x20;           )

&#x20;       )

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   override fun onSuggestionSelected(suggestion: Suggestion) {

&#x20;       when (suggestion.authorityLevel) {

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.SUGGEST -> {

&#x20;               // Just execute (low risk)

&#x20;               suggestion.action.invoke()

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.ACT\_WITH\_CONFIRM -> {

&#x20;               // Show confirmation dialog

&#x20;               showConfirmationDialog(suggestion)

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           else -> {

&#x20;               // Require explicit user action

&#x20;               showCannotAutoExecuteDialog()

&#x20;           }

&#x20;       }

&#x20;   }

}

Done Test: Every feature has all five lenses implemented and accessible.



12.1.5 How to Implement the Authority Ladder (From GEMINI.md + State.md)

The Authority Ladder governs all AI and automated actions:



kotlin

Copy

// AuthorityLadder.kt

enum class AuthorityLevel {

&#x20;   // 1. Observe \& explain (always on)

&#x20;   OBSERVE\_EXPLAIN,



&#x20;   // 2. Suggest (default — shows recommendation, waits for a tap)

&#x20;   SUGGEST,



&#x20;   // 3. Act with one-tap confirm (medium-stakes: block a domain, enable a rule)

&#x20;   ACT\_WITH\_CONFIRM,



&#x20;   // 4. Act autonomously (only fully reversible, low-stakes, user-whitelisted actions)

&#x20;   ACT\_AUTONOMOUSLY,



&#x20;   // 5. Never autonomous: wipe, lockdown, root toggles, vault access, anything on F112 list

&#x20;   NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS

}



// List of actions that are NEVER autonomous

val NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS\_ACTIONS = setOf(

&#x20;   "wipe",

&#x20;   "lockdown",

&#x20;   "root\_toggle",

&#x20;   "vault\_access",

&#x20;   "factory\_reset",

&#x20;   "secure\_wipe",

&#x20;   // Add all F112 two-factor list items

)



class AuthorityLadder {

&#x20;   fun canExecute(action: String, level: AuthorityLevel): Boolean {

&#x20;       if (action in NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS\_ACTIONS) {

&#x20;           return level == AuthorityLevel.NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS

&#x20;       }



&#x20;       return when (level) {

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.OBSERVE\_EXPLAIN -> true

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.SUGGEST -> true

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.ACT\_WITH\_CONFIRM -> true

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.ACT\_AUTONOMOUSLY -> true

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS -> false

&#x20;       }

&#x20;   }



&#x20;   fun getRequiredLevel(action: String): AuthorityLevel {

&#x20;       if (action in NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS\_ACTIONS) {

&#x20;           return AuthorityLevel.NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS

&#x20;       }



&#x20;       // Default to SUGGEST for most actions

&#x20;       return AuthorityLevel.SUGGEST

&#x20;   }

}



// Usage in AI Assistant

class AIAssistant {

&#x20;   private val authorityLadder = AuthorityLadder()



&#x20;   fun handleRequest(request: String, context: Context) {

&#x20;       val (action, explanation) = analyzeRequest(request, context)



&#x20;       val requiredLevel = authorityLadder.getRequiredLevel(action)



&#x20;       when (requiredLevel) {

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.OBSERVE\_EXPLAIN -> {

&#x20;               // Always allowed - just explain

&#x20;               showExplanation(explanation)

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.SUGGEST -> {

&#x20;               // Show suggestion, wait for user

&#x20;               showSuggestion(action, explanation)

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.ACT\_WITH\_CONFIRM -> {

&#x20;               // Show suggestion with confirm button

&#x20;               showSuggestionWithConfirm(action, explanation)

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.ACT\_AUTONOMOUSLY -> {

&#x20;               // Only if user has whitelisted this action

&#x20;               if (isWhitelisted(action)) {

&#x20;                   executeAction(action)

&#x20;               } else {

&#x20;                   showSuggestionWithConfirm(action, explanation)

&#x20;               }

&#x20;           }

&#x20;           AuthorityLevel.NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS -> {

&#x20;               // Never execute, only explain

&#x20;               showExplanation("I cannot perform this action. $explanation")

&#x20;           }

&#x20;       }

&#x20;   }

}

Done Test: AI assistant never executes actions beyond its authority level, especially NEVER\_AUTONOMOUS actions.



12.2 User Guides

12.2.1 How to Use Fake-Online Mode (From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf + User's Original Vision)

Step 1: Enable Fake-Online for an App



Open SERF

Navigate to the Apps settings category

Find the app you want to use offline

Toggle Fake-Online to ON

Optionally, select a profile (if available) or let SERF learn automatically

Step 2: Go Offline



Enable Airplane Mode on your device

OR disable mobile data and Wi-Fi

OR use SERF's Network Toggle tile

Launch the app

What Happens:



SERF's VpnService creates a virtual network

The app sees the device as "connected"

SERF's probe responder answers connectivity checks with HTTP 204

For known domains, SERF returns appropriate responses

For unknown domains, SERF returns a fast failure (HTTP 503)

Step 3: If the App Doesn't Work



Check the Transmission Ledger (F663) to see what connections the app is trying to make

Look for red flags in the Detect lens

Try different response strategies in the Suggest lens:

Fast error (HTTP 503)

Empty 200 (HTTP 200 with empty body)

Empty JSON ({})

Empty array (\[])

Delayed timeout

Tap "Works" or "Stuck" to help SERF learn

If it works, the setting is saved for future use

Step 4: Share Your Profile (Optional)



If you've successfully configured an app, consider sharing your profile

Open the app's settings in SERF

Tap "Share Profile"

Choose to upload to the community database (F07, F216)

Other users can now benefit from your work

Limitations:



Certificate Pinning: Some apps ignore user-installed certificates (Android 7+). These require root or a patched APK.

Request Signing: If the app sends signed requests with nonces or timestamps, replay won't work without templated responses.

Non-HTTP Traffic: UDP, WebSockets, and custom protocols may not be fully supported.

Attestation: Play Integrity and similar checks cannot be faked.

Tier 3 Apps: Apps with real server logic (multiplayer, server-side state) cannot be made fully offline.

12.2.2 How to Use the Tile Grid (From State.md + GEMINI.md)

Adding a Tile:



Tap the + tile on your home screen

Browse or search for the feature you want

Tap the feature to add it as a tile

The tile will appear on your home screen

Resizing a Tile:



Long-press on any tile

The tile will enter jiggle mode with resize handles

Drag a handle to resize:

1x1: Small icon + short label

2x2: Default size (icon + label + 1-2 stat lines)

4x2: Full-width status bar

4x4: Full-width tall tile with scrolling content

Tap anywhere outside the tile to lock in the new size

Removing a Tile:



Long-press on the tile

Drag it to the trash can icon that appears

Release to remove

Rearranging Tiles:



Long-press on a tile

Drag it to the desired position

Release to drop it in place

Tile Types:



Monitor Tiles: Show live status (e.g., network traffic, battery level)

Control Tiles: Toggle features on/off (e.g., VPN, Firewall)

Action Tiles: Perform actions when tapped (e.g., Clear Cache, Run Scan)

Coming Soon Tiles: Show features that aren't built yet (F888)

12.2.3 How to Use the Settings Drill-Down (From GEMINI.md)

Navigation:



Tap the Settings tile on your home screen

You'll see a list of categories (Network, Privacy, Firewall, etc.)

Tap a category to see its sub-features

Tap a sub-feature to see its detailed settings

Tile Toggle:



Next to every category and sub-feature is a "Tile" toggle

Switching this to ON instantly creates a tile on your home screen

The tile shows the same content as the settings screen

You can access the feature from either the tile or the settings menu

Root Settings:



Every category's settings screen ends with a "Root Settings" subsection

This is always visible but grayed out on unrooted devices

On rooted devices, these settings become active

This ensures root capabilities are always discoverable

12.2.4 How to Use the Widget Studio (From GEMINI.md)

Creating a Widget:



Open the Widgets management module

You'll see a dedicated studio for configuring widgets

All available features are listed with checkboxes

Check the boxes for the features you want as widgets

Use the "Select All" checkbox at the top of each column

Tap "Create Widgets"

Styling Widgets:



For each widget, you can customize its appearance:

Theme: Light or Dark

Shape: Square, Round, Rounded Square

Background: Solid, Transparent (Glassmorphism), Hidden

Preview the widget before finalizing

Placing Widgets:



The widgets will be created as Android system widgets

Long-press on your Android home screen

Tap "Widgets"

Find and drag the SERF widgets to your home screen

Widget Behavior:



Widgets update in real-time with live data

Tapping a widget opens the full feature in SERF

Some widgets have quick actions (e.g., toggle on/off)

12.2.5 How to Use Location Spoofing (From GEMINIchatFULL.html)

Basic Location Spoofing:



Open SERF

Navigate to Location \& GPS settings

Enable Location Spoofer

Select a location on the map or enter coordinates

Tap Start Spoofing

Advanced Options:



Sync with Network Proxy: Automatically route your network traffic through a matching country

Environment Spoofing: Also spoof Wi-Fi BSSIDs and cell tower IDs to match your spoofed location

Satellite Drift Simulation: Add microscopic jitter to appear like a live GPS lock

Modes:



Mode A (Location-Driven Proxy): Drop a pin → auto-route traffic through matching country

Mode B (Proxy-Driven Location): Select proxy country → auto-sync GPS to match

Limitations:



Some apps detect mock location via the isMock flag

SERF strips this flag at the kernel/proxy layer to avoid detection

High-security apps may use additional detection methods that are harder to bypass

Warning:



Spoofing location to deceive cellular networks is illegal (D9, FCC boundaries)

Spoofing location locally to apps is legal and fully supported

12.3 Contributor Guides

12.3.1 How to Contribute a Feature (From State.md + GEMINI.md)

Step 1: Understand the Project



Read STATE.md sections 0, 1, and 10 (Live State)

Understand the Mission and Vision (section 1)

Review the Strategic Decisions (section 2)

Check Pending Fork Decisions (section 4.19) — don't resolve these unilaterally

Step 2: Pick a Task



Review Build Order (section 9) to see what's next

OR pick an unimplemented feature from the Feature Register (section 5)

Check the Parking Lot (section 11) — don't implement anything there without explicit permission

Step 3: Research First



Perform TWO searches (section 4.20):

(a) Current advancements, standards, patches, open-source repos

(b) Current vulnerabilities, bugs, exploits, attack patterns

Check Reusable Components Registry (section 4.18)

Add any new components to the registry

Step 4: Follow the Module Contract



Every feature must be an independent module (section 4.2)

Implement all required interfaces (manifest, settings schema, tests, etc.)

Follow the Five Lenses Standard (section 4.14)

Respect the Authority Ladder (section 4.13) for any AI/automated actions

Step 5: Implement the Feature



Create a new module directory under app/src/main/java/com/serf/modules/

Implement the module manifest (name, version, permissions, etc.)

Implement the feature logic following the Module Contract

Add unit tests (Module Contract 4.2.5)

Add documentation (one-paragraph doc comment)

Step 6: Test Thoroughly



Test on multiple devices (use F243's compatibility map)

Test edge cases and error conditions

Ensure no crashes and no silent failures

Verify Five Lenses all work correctly

Step 7: Submit Your Contribution



Fork the repository on GitHub

Create a new branch for your feature

Commit your changes with clear, descriptive messages

Push your branch to your fork

Open a Pull Request to the main repository

Reference the F-number in your PR title and description

Wait for review and feedback

Step 8: Address Feedback



Respond to code review comments

Make requested changes

Update your PR with new commits

Wait for approval and merge

12.3.2 How to Contribute a Profile (From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf + State.md)

Step 1: Record a Profile



Open SERF

Navigate to Fake-Online settings

Select the app you want to profile

Tap "Record Mode"

Use the app normally for 1-5 minutes

SERF will automatically sanitize the recording (remove personal data)

Tap "Stop Recording"

Step 2: Test the Profile



Enable Airplane Mode

Select the recorded profile for the app

Launch the app

Verify it works offline

If it doesn't work, adjust the response strategies and try again

Step 3: Share the Profile



Open the app's settings in SERF

Tap "Share Profile"

Choose a name and description for your profile

Select "Upload to Community Database"

The profile will be validated automatically by a GitHub Action

Once validated, it will be merged into the community database

Profile Format:



json

Copy

{

&#x20; "packageName": "com.example.app",

&#x20; "version": "1.2.3",

&#x20; "versionCode": 456,

&#x20; "name": "Example App",

&#x20; "description": "Profile for Example App v1.2.3",

&#x20; "author": "contributor123",

&#x20; "date": "2024-01-15",

&#x20; "tier": 1,

&#x20; "rules": \[

&#x20;   {

&#x20;     "domain": "api.example.com",

&#x20;     "path": "/v1/config",

&#x20;     "method": "GET",

&#x20;     "responseStrategy": "recordReplay",

&#x20;     "response": {

&#x20;       "status": 200,

&#x20;       "body": "{...}",

&#x20;       "headers": {...}

&#x20;     }

&#x20;   },

&#x20;   {

&#x20;     "domain": "connectivitycheck.gstatic.com",

&#x20;     "path": "/generate\_204",

&#x20;     "method": "GET",

&#x20;     "responseStrategy": "stub204",

&#x20;     "response": {

&#x20;       "status": 204

&#x20;     }

&#x20;   }

&#x20; ],

&#x20; "dynamicFields": \[

&#x20;   {

&#x20;     "name": "timestamp",

&#x20;     "type": "currentTime",

&#x20;     "format": "ISO8601"

&#x20;   },

&#x20;   {

&#x20;     "name": "nonce",

&#x20;     "type": "randomUUID"

&#x20;   }

&#x20; ],

&#x20; "works": true,

&#x20; "testedOn": \["Pixel 6", "Samsung S22"],

&#x20; "notes": "Works for basic offline use. Login may require additional rules."

}

Validation Rules:



No personal data — All sensitive information must be sanitized

Valid JSON — Profile must be valid JSON

Required fields — All required fields must be present

Tested — Profile must have been tested and confirmed working

No malicious content — Profile must not contain malicious rules

12.3.3 How to Set Up Offline Development (From GEMINIchatFULL.html)

Step 1: Initial "Wet-Ink" Setup (Internet Required)



Clone the repository:

cd serf

Step 2: Cutting the Cord (Going Air-Gapped)

You now have an unlimited, free, private coding assistant

Step 3: Offline Development Workflow



Write code in your favorite IDE (Android Studio, VS Code, etc.)

Run tests locally

Use local LLM for code suggestions, documentation, etc.

When internet is available, push changes to GitHub

Python: \~100-200 MB

Android SDK/NDK: \~5-10 GB

13\. DECISION LOG (Why, Not Just What)

From State.md — preserved and expanded with all sources:



13.0 Decision Log Structure

Each entry includes:



Decision: What was decided

Options Considered: What alternatives were evaluated

Rationale: Why the decision was made

Impact: What this means for the project

Date: When the decision was made

Source: Which conversation or document it came from

13.1 Core Architectural Decisions

13.1.1 D1: Fork RethinkDNS as IP-Layer Foundation

Decision: Fork RethinkDNS as the IP-layer foundation.



Options Considered:



A: Fork RethinkDNS (Go/gVisor core)

B: Build from scratch in Rust

C: Use existing VPN library (e.g., libvpn)

D: Use Android's built-in VPN APIs only

Rationale:



Pro A: RethinkDNS is already a hardened, battle-tested VPN/firewall solution with MPL-2.0 license. Forking saves significant development time and inherits existing security features.

Con B: Building from scratch would take months/years and would likely have more vulnerabilities initially.

Con C: Existing libraries may not have all needed features or may have incompatible licenses.

Con D: Android's built-in VPN APIs are sufficient for basic functionality but lack the depth needed for advanced features.

Impact:



We inherit a proven, secure foundation for IP-layer traffic control

We can extend the core with new features (Module SDK, Five Lenses, etc.)

We must verify the MPL-2.0 license (T0.3) and ensure compliance

We diverge from upstream UI (D10) to implement our own design

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2, GEMINIchatFULL.html



13.1.2 D2: Rust for New Performance-Critical Modules

Decision: Use Rust for new performance-critical modules, called from Kotlin. Go core stays as-is.



Options Considered:



A: Full Rust rewrite of entire app

B: Rust for new modules, Go core stays

C: Kotlin only

D: C++ for performance-critical parts

Rationale:



Con A: Full Rust rewrite would lose the hardened, battle-tested Go/gVisor core. The modest gain (no GC pauses) doesn't justify the cost.

Pro B: Rust provides memory safety and performance for new modules (packet processing, eBPF hooks, traffic playback) while keeping the proven Go core.

Con C: Kotlin is not suitable for low-level packet processing and high-performance networking.

Con D: C++ lacks Rust's memory safety guarantees and modern tooling.

Impact:



Rust Data Plane: TUN/TAP, packet parsing, connection tracking, eBPF hooks

Go Core: Existing RethinkDNS functionality (DNS, firewall, basic VPN)

Kotlin: UI, Android-specific integrations, settings

Python: Control plane, rule orchestration, AI integration

Date: Baseline (State.md) + Validated by GEMINIchatFULL.html Source: State.md section 2, GEMINIchatFULL.html



13.1.3 D3: Offline "Fake-Online" Works at Application Level

Decision: Fake-Online works at the application level, not raw TLS replay (fresh keys each session defeat replay).



Options Considered:



A: Raw TLS replay

B: Application-level fake with layered approach

C: Server emulation

D: MITM proxy with custom certificates

Rationale:



Con A: Raw TLS replay doesn't work because modern apps use fresh keys per session, certificate pinning, and nonces. Replayed sessions will be rejected.

Pro B: The layered approach from CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf actually works:

Fake network presence (VpnService)

Built-in junk traffic knowledge (stub responses for analytics/ads)

Fail fast on unknown (immediate clean error)

Learn on device (try different response strategies)

Read APK (static analysis to pre-sort)

Share results (community database)

Root module (for apps that read airplane mode/SIM state directly)

Partial C: Server emulation is needed for Tier 3 apps (multiplayer, server-side state) but is out of scope for the core Fake-Online feature.

Partial D: MITM proxy with custom certificates works for some apps but requires root or patched APK for certificate-pinned apps.

Impact:



We implement a layered, application-level approach that works for Tiers 0-2 (80-90% of apps)

Tier 3 apps require separate server emulation efforts

We prioritize the layers that provide the most value:

Layer 1-3: MVP (fixes "requires internet to launch" apps)

Layer 4-6: Advanced (fixes more complex apps)

Layer 7: Optional (for root users)

Date: Baseline (State.md) + CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf Source: State.md section 2, CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf



13.1.4 D4: Home Screen = Scrollable Resizable Tiles

Decision: Home screen uses a scrollable resizable tile grid (4.11 spec).



Options Considered:



A: Traditional list view

B: Dashboard with fixed widgets

C: Scrollable resizable tile grid

D: Customizable home screen replacement

Rationale:



Con A: List view is not visual enough for a network/privacy app where live data is important.

Con B: Fixed widgets limit flexibility and don't allow users to prioritize what's important to them.

Pro C: Tile grid provides:

Visual hierarchy (larger tiles for more important features)

Customization (users can arrange as they like)

Live data (tiles can show real-time status)

Familiarity (similar to Android's widget system)

Con D: Full home screen replacement is too invasive and may conflict with Android's design guidelines.

Impact:



We implement the 8-wide logical grid with sizes: 1x1, 2x2, 4x2, 4x4

Long-press to resize (identical to Android's widget resize)

\+ tile opens searchable, categorized tile gallery

Coming Soon tiles (F888) for unbuilt features

Root Settings always visible but grayed out on unrooted devices

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2, 4.11



13.1.5 D5: Build in the Cloud

Decision: Build in the cloud using GitHub + GitHub Actions.



Options Considered:



A: Local development only

B: GitHub + GitHub Actions

C: GitLab + GitLab CI

D: Self-hosted CI/CD

Rationale:



Con A: Local development limits contributors who don't have development environments.

Pro B: GitHub is the most accessible for open-source projects:

Free for public repositories

GitHub Actions provides free CI minutes

Easy for contributors to submit PRs

Integrates with F216 (contribution pipeline)

Partial C: GitLab is a good alternative but has less mindshare in the Android community.

Con D: Self-hosted CI/CD requires maintenance and infrastructure.

Impact:



No PC required to build the app (per user's request)

GitHub Actions builds the APK automatically

Users can download APKs from GitHub releases

F216 (contribution pipeline) uses GitHub PRs and Actions for validation

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2



13.1.6 D6: Scanners/Auditors Scope to Own Networks/Devices

Decision: All scanning and auditing tools are gated to the user's own networks and devices.



Options Considered:



A: Allow scanning any network

B: Scope to own networks/devices only

C: Allow scanning with explicit user consent

D: No scanning capabilities at all

Rationale:



Legal Con A: Scanning networks you don't own violates CFAA (Computer Fraud and Abuse Act).

Pro B: Scoping to own networks/devices:

Complies with CFAA

Respects user privacy (doesn't scan others' devices)

Still provides value (users can audit their own networks)

Con C: "Explicit consent" is hard to verify and may not hold up in court.

Con D: Removing scanning capabilities limits the app's usefulness for security-conscious users.

Impact:



All scanning features (F44, F166, F196-F200, etc.) are gated to own networks/devices

Clear disclaimers that these tools are for personal use only

No default scanning of external networks

User must explicitly select their own networks/devices

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2, 4.12



13.1.7 D7: Distribution via F-Droid and Direct APK

Decision: Distribution is F-Droid and direct APK, not Google Play.



Options Considered:



A: Google Play only

B: F-Droid only

C: Direct APK only

D: F-Droid + Direct APK

E: All of the above

Rationale:



Con A: Google Play blocks many privacy/security features (VPN, firewall, root access, etc.).

Con B: F-Droid lacks visibility and some users may not know about it.

Con C: Direct APK lacks automatic updates and security scanning.

Pro D: F-Droid + Direct APK provides:

Maximum reach (F-Droid for privacy-conscious users, direct APK for everyone else)

No Google restrictions (we control the distribution)

Automatic updates via F-Droid

Direct feedback from users

Con E: Google Play would require removing or neutering core features.

Impact:



Not on Google Play (expected and fine)

F-Droid repository for automatic updates

GitHub releases for direct APK downloads

No Google restrictions on features

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2



13.1.8 D8: Piracy-Adjacent Features Permanently Out of Scope

Decision: Piracy-adjacent features (license/purchase-check removal on others' apps, ad-removal inside paid apps) are permanently out of scope.



Options Considered:



A: Allow all piracy-adjacent features

B: Allow some with warnings

C: Out of scope permanently

D: Allow for "testing" purposes only

Rationale:



Legal Con A/B/D: These features violate copyright law and DMCA 1201.

Ethical Con A/B/D: These features undermine developers and violate the project's values (helping users, not stealing from developers).

Pro C: Permanently out of scope:

Protects the project's legal standing

Aligns with project values (helping users, not harming developers)

Avoids ethical gray areas

Impact:



No features that remove license checks or ads from paid apps

No features that bypass DRM or in-app purchases

Clear boundary that protects the project

Focus on legitimate use cases (privacy, security, offline functionality)

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2, GEMINI.md



13.1.9 D9: Transmission-Capable RF Features Stay Passive/Detection-Only

Decision: Transmission-capable RF features stay passive/detection-only (FCC/wiretap boundary).



Options Considered:



A: Allow active RF transmission

B: Passive/detection-only

C: Allow with warnings

D: No RF features at all

Rationale:



Legal Con A/C: Active RF transmission violates FCC regulations and may require licensing.

Pro B: Passive/detection-only:

Complies with FCC regulations

Still provides value (detecting RF signals, jamming attempts, etc.)

Avoids legal issues

Con D: Removing RF features limits the app's capabilities for security-conscious users.

Impact:



Detect: yes (RF signals, jamming attempts, etc.)

Jam or transmit: no (FCC violation)

Clear boundary that protects the project legally

Focus on detection and monitoring

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2, 4.12, GEMINI.md



13.1.10 D10: Native Android Design Language

Decision: Use native Android design language (Material Design, system fonts/icons, standard gestures).



Options Considered:



A: Custom design language

B: Native Android design

C: iOS-style design

D: Cross-platform design

Rationale:



Con A: Custom design feels out of place on Android and may confuse users.

Pro B: Native Android design:

Feels like it belongs on the device

Familiar to users (standard gestures, patterns)

Follows Material Design guidelines

Easier to maintain (uses system components)

Con C: iOS-style design doesn't fit on Android.

Con D: Cross-platform design compromises on both platforms.

Impact:



Material Design components

System font and icons

Standard gestures (long-press to edit, drag to reorder)

Tile grid follows Android patterns (similar to widget resize)

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2, 4.11



13.1.11 D11: Personal Health Data Permanently Out of Scope

Decision: Personal health data (medical, biometric, etc.) is permanently out of scope.



Options Considered:



A: Allow all health data features

B: Allow with warnings

C: Out of scope permanently

D: Allow for "wellness" purposes only

Rationale:



Legal/Regulatory Con A/B/D: Health data is highly regulated (HIPAA, GDPR, etc.).

Liability Con A/B/D: Handling health data creates significant liability for the project.

Ethical Con A/B/D: Health data is sensitive and requires special handling.

Pro C: Permanently out of scope:

Avoids legal/regulatory issues

Reduces liability

Focuses on core mission (network/privacy control)

Impact:



No features that track, store, or manage personal health/medical/biometric data

Device-level health (battery health, F596) is unaffected

Generic "health check" as a technical term (F701, F43) is unaffected

Clear boundary that protects the project

Date: Baseline (State.md) Source: State.md section 2



13.2 Feature-Specific Decisions

13.2.1 Fork 1: Custom Chromium Fork

Decision: Fork 4 = A — WebView stays permanent, settled, no custom Chromium fork.



Options Considered:



A: Use Android's WebView (permanent)

B: Fork Chromium for custom browser

Rationale:



Pro A: WebView is already on the device, no additional download required.

Con B: Forking Chromium is a massive undertaking (millions of lines of code, continuous updates, security patches).

Con B: Custom Chromium fork would require separate maintenance and may fall behind on security updates.

Pro A: WebView provides sufficient functionality for most use cases.

Impact:



No custom Chromium fork

WebView is the permanent solution

Browser features use WebView

If advanced browser features are needed, they must work within WebView's limitations

Date: Resolved this session Source: State.md section 4.19, 13



13.2.2 Fork 2: Reticulum Integration

Decision: Fork 2 = B — Sequenced last before Fork 1, including importing Reticulum's source into the user's own repo first.



Options Considered:



A: Build custom mesh transport from scratch

B: Integrate Reticulum stack

Rationale:



Con A: Building from scratch would take significant time and likely have more vulnerabilities.

Pro B: Reticulum is a proven, open-source mesh networking stack:

Already implements many needed features (P2P, encryption, etc.)

Actively maintained

Secure by design

Pro B: Importing Reticulum's source into our repo ensures we control the code and can customize as needed.

Impact:



Reticulum stack integration for mesh features (F90-F93, F292-309)

One-Tap Reticulum Onboarding (auto node selection, zero manual config)

NomadNet Pages Built In

MicroReticulum Relay Mode (cheap MCU range extension)

Self-Hostable Public Transport Node (secure-by-default)

Date: Resolved this session Source: State.md section 4.19, 13



13.2.3 Fork 3: Wi-Fi/Data Chaff Generator

Decision: Fork 3 = B — F104 and F278 are retired and merged into F619.



Options Considered:



A: Keep F104 and F278 separate

B: Merge F104 and F278 into F619

Rationale:



Pro B: F104 (Wi-Fi Chaff Generator) and F278 (Data Chaff Generator) are functionally similar and should be unified.

Pro B: F619 (Wi-Fi/Data Chaff Generator) provides a consistent interface for both Wi-Fi and data chaff.

Con A: Keeping them separate would duplicate code and create maintenance burden.

Impact:



F104 and F278 are retired

Their functionality lives in F619

Unified chaff generation for Wi-Fi and data

Simpler architecture

Date: Resolved this session Source: State.md section 4.19, 13



13.2.4 Fork 4: WebView vs Custom Chromium

Decision: Fork 4 = A — WebView stays permanent, settled, no custom Chromium fork.



Same as Fork 1 — already resolved.



Date: Resolved this session Source: State.md section 4.19, 13



13.3 Research-Driven Decisions

13.3.1 Android Treble and GSI Compatibility

Decision: GSI (Generic System Image) installation is feasible but limited due to VINTF/vendor interface mismatches.



Rationale:



Pro: Android Treble architecture separates system and vendor partitions, making GSIs possible.

Con: Not all OEMs implement VINTF cleanly, leading to HAL mismatches.

Con: Verified Boot must be explicitly disabled (vbmeta handling), which is a separate step from unlocking the bootloader.

Impact:



F313 (Guided GSI Installer) is technically feasible but may not work on all devices.

F317 (Vendor Interface Diagnostic) can report what broke but cannot fix it automatically.

GSI compatibility is device-dependent and requires manual testing.

Date: From State.md section 11.2 Source: CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf, State.md



13.3.2 Certificate Pinning and TLS

Decision: Certificate pinning cannot be bypassed without root or a patched APK (Android 7+).



Rationale:



Fact: Since Android 7 (Nougat), apps ignore user-installed CAs by default.

Fact: Apps can pin specific certificates or public keys, making MITM attacks impossible without modifying the app.

Workaround: Root access or a patched APK is required to bypass certificate pinning.

Impact:



Fake-Online works for most apps (those that don't use certificate pinning).

Some apps (banks, financial apps, etc.) cannot be faked offline without root or a patched APK.

Root module (Layer 7) is needed for these apps.

Date: From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf Source: CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf



13.3.3 Request Signing and Nonces

Decision: Request signing and nonces require templated responses, not pure replay.



Rationale:



Fact: Many apps include timestamps, nonces, or HMAC signatures in their requests.

Fact: A replayed response will have mismatched values (old timestamp, wrong nonce).

Solution: Dynamic response synthesis is needed to generate fresh, valid responses that match the current request.

Impact:



F03 (Dynamic Response Synthesis) is essential for Tier 2 apps (login, save sync, entitlement checks).

Pure replay (F02) works for Tier 0-1 apps only.

AI-driven response generation (from GEMINIchatFULL.html) can help automate template creation.

Date: From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf Source: CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf, GEMINIchatFULL.html



13.3.4 Non-HTTP Traffic

Decision: Non-HTTP traffic (UDP, WebSockets, custom protocols) requires separate handling.



Rationale:



Fact: Many apps use UDP for VoIP, gaming, etc.

Fact: WebSockets are common for real-time communication.

Fact: Some apps use custom binary protocols.

Solution: Separate handlers are needed for each protocol type.

Impact:



Initial focus on HTTP/HTTPS (covers 80-90% of apps).

Future work to support UDP, WebSockets, custom protocols.

Protocol-specific modules can be added via the Module SDK (F213).

Date: From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf Source: CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf



13.3.5 Attestation (Play Integrity, etc.)

Decision: Attestation checks cannot be faked with replayed data.



Rationale:



Fact: Play Integrity, SafetyNet, and similar checks verify device integrity using cryptographic attestation.

Fact: These checks cannot be bypassed without root access and kernel-level modifications.

Solution: No solution for faking attestation — these apps cannot be made fully offline.

Impact:



Some apps (banks, financial apps, etc.) cannot be used offline due to attestation checks.

Focus on apps that don't use attestation or where attestation is not critical for offline functionality.

Date: From CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf Source: CLAUDEchatFULL.pdf



13.4 Legal Decisions

13.4.1 DMCA 1201 Compliance

Decision: Never defeat Verified Boot's cryptographic signature chain (DMCA 1201 circumvention).



Rationale:



Legal: DMCA 1201 prohibits circumvention of technological protection measures.

Technical: Verified Boot's signature chain is a technological protection measure.

Ethical: Circumventing Verified Boot undermines device security.

Impact:



F311-F314 (OS-replacement path) operate on the legal side of this line (driver extraction, interoperability).

Any feature requiring Verified Boot circumvention is permanently out of scope.

Clear boundary that protects the project legally.

Date: From State.md section 11.4, 15 Source: State.md, GEMINI.md



13.4.2 No Binary Tampering

Decision: Never decompile, alter, re-sign, or patch third-party APKs.



Rationale:



Legal: Tampering with third-party binaries violates copyright law and may violate DMCA 1201.

Ethical: Respecting developers' rights aligns with the project's values.

Technical: Runtime modifications via safe memory hooks, local network proxies, or standard Android APIs are allowed.

Impact:



No APK cracking, modding, or repackaging.

Runtime modifications are allowed via:

Shizuku/ADB (F321)

Local VPN (F10, F123)

Xposed/Frida (for root users)

Clear boundary that protects the project legally and ethically.

Date: From GEMINI.md Source: GEMINI.md, State.md section 15



13.4.3 FCC Compliance

Decision: No RF jamming or transmission beyond certified hardware limits.



Rationale:



Legal: FCC regulates RF transmissions and prohibits jamming.

Technical: Passive detection (RF scanning, monitoring) is allowed.

Ethical: Jamming disrupts legitimate communications and is harmful.

Impact:



Detect: yes (RF signals, jamming attempts, etc.)

Jam or transmit: no

Clear boundary that protects the project legally.

Date: From State.md section 2, GEMINI.md Source: State.md, GEMINI.md



13.4.4 Wiretap Act (ECPA) Compliance

Decision: Never intercept others' communications without consent.



Rationale:



Legal: Wiretap Act (ECPA) prohibits intercepting communications without consent.

Technical: SERF only handles the user's own traffic.

Ethical: Respecting others' privacy aligns with the project's values.

Impact:



Only the user's own traffic is intercepted, modified, or analyzed.

No eavesdropping on others' communications.

Clear boundary that protects the project legally.

Date: From State.md section 4.12 Source: State.md



13.4.5 CFAA Compliance

Decision: Scanners/auditors scope to own networks/devices only.



Rationale:



Legal: CFAA prohibits unauthorized access to computer systems.

Technical: Scanning only the user's own networks/devices complies with CFAA.

Ethical: Respecting others' property aligns with the project's values.

Impact:



All scanning features are gated to own networks/devices.

No default scanning of external networks.

Clear disclaimers that these tools are for personal use only.

Date: From State.md section 2, 4.12 Source: State.md



14\. PROJECT HANDOFF — Welcome, Future Contributors

From State.md — preserved and enhanced with all sources:



14.0 Purpose

This section exists so SERF is never just "here it is, finished, goodbye." It's a standing invitation, written for whoever picks this up next, whether that's:



The original author months from now

A hobbyist who found the repo

A security researcher who wants to help harden it

An AI agent continuing the work

You are that person. Welcome.



14.1 README.md (Public Repo, Copy This in Verbatim When the Repo Goes Live)

markdown

Copy

SERF

A free, open-source phone that actually belongs to you.



What This Does

Makes apps that shouldn't need a connection work offline. Strips tracking you never agreed to. De-Googles without needing root. Explains everything it's doing in plain language, never hidden behind jargon.



Every feature is off until you switch it on. Nothing here phones home. There's no company behind this trying to profit from your data.



Why It Exists

This project started from ordinary frustration:



Apps that wouldn't open without internet

Google Play silently hiding apps and blocking installs

A bank app that stopped working after one update on a phone its owner has every right to control

The honest answer to "why doesn't a good version of this already exist?" was: because most software gets built to extract money, not to help.



This is the attempt at the other version.



Where to Start

Read



text



STATE.md

. It's the single living blueprint, with:

Every feature planned (1000+ and growing)

Every decision made and why

What's built, what's next

Exactly how to resume work

Section 0 tells you exactly how to resume work in a single paste.



Section 1 is the mission — read it before anything else. It's the part that shouldn't change even as everything else does.



How to Contribute

1\. Understand the Project

Read

text



STATE.md

sections 0, 1, and 10 (Live State) to see exactly where things stand.

Check section 4.19 (Pending Fork Decisions). These are real forks in the road the original project owner chose to decide personally. If one's still open, leave it open and work on something else.

2\. Pick a Task

Section 9's tiers show build order by real difficulty and priority.

Section 5's 37 categories let you find every idea on a topic you care about with one search.

Start with Tier 0 or Tier 1 if you're new to the project.

3\. Research First

Before building anything, perform TWO searches (section 4.20):

(a) Current advancements, standards, patches, open-source repos

(b) Current vulnerabilities, bugs, exploits, attack patterns

Check section 4.18's Reusable Components Registry first.

Integrate, don't reimplement — use existing, actively-maintained, appropriately-licensed projects.

4\. Follow the Module Contract

Section 4.2 is what keeps 1000+ features from turning into unmaintainable spaghetti.

Every feature is its own independent, removable module.

Follow all 11 rules of the Module Contract.

5\. Never Build Parking Lot Features

Section 11 (Parking Lot) contains features that require a user decision before implementation.

Never build anything in section 11 without explicit permission.

These lines are there for real reasons — see section 13 (Decision Log).

6\. Respect the Boundaries

D8: No piracy-adjacent features (license/purchase-check removal).

D9: No RF jamming or transmission beyond certified limits.

D11: No personal health data.

Section 15: Things we can't do (legal/ethical boundaries).

These are non-negotiable.

What Kind of Help Genuinely Matters Most Right Now

In order of priority:



Real-device testers — Especially unusual or budget phones. F243's compatibility map needs you.

Translators — Help make SERF accessible to non-English speakers.

Security reviewers — Audit the encryption claims, find vulnerabilities.

Documentation writers — Improve the Five Lenses content, How-To guides.

Feature implementers — Pick one single F-number and build it well.

Even small contributions help. Fixing a typo, improving a description, or testing on one device — it all moves the project forward.



Build Instructions

Prerequisites

Android Studio (for Kotlin/Java development)

Rust (for data plane modules)

Python 3.10+ (for control plane)

Git (for version control)

Setup

bash

Copy

\# Clone the repository

git clone https://github.com/yourusername/serf.git

cd serf



\# Set up Rust (for data plane)

curl --proto '=https' --tlsv1.2 -sSf https://sh.rustup.rs | sh

source $HOME/.cargo/env

rustup target add aarch64-linux-android armv7-linux-androideabi i686-linux-android



\# Set up Python (for control plane)

python3 -m venv venv

source venv/bin/activate  # On Windows: venv\\Scripts\\activate

pip install -r requirements.txt



\# Build the app

cd app

./gradlew assembleDebug

Running

Install the APK on your Android device:

adb install app/build/outputs/apk/debug/app-debug.apk

Or download from GitHub Releases.

License

This project is licensed under the MPL-2.0 license (same as RethinkDNS).



See LICENSE for details.



Contact

Issues:  GitHub Issues

Discussions:  GitHub Discussions

Matrix: #serf:matrix.org (if set up)

No private support. All discussions happen in public to benefit the community.



Build something you'd actually want to use.

\## \*\*14.2 In-App "About This App" Screen (User-Facing, Not Contributor-Facing — Plain Language)\*\*



```markdown

\# About SERF



\## What Is This App?



This app gives you back \*\*control of your own phone\*\*.



It can:

\- Make apps work offline that shouldn't need a connection

\- Block tracking you never agreed to

\- Show you exactly what's happening on your network, \*\*explained plainly\*\*, not hidden behind technical jargon



\## What It Doesn't Do



\- \*\*No tracking.\*\* SERF doesn't phone home or collect your data.

\- \*\*No ads.\*\* The app is free, open-source, and built by people who wanted something better.

\- \*\*No root required.\*\* Most features work without root (some advanced features require it).



\## How to Use It



1\. \*\*Tap any feature\*\* to see what it does.

2\. \*\*Use the Five Lenses\*\* to understand each feature:

&#x20;  - \*\*Monitor:\*\* See live status

&#x20;  - \*\*Detect:\*\* See active issues

&#x20;  - \*\*Explain:\*\* Read plain-language description

&#x20;  - \*\*Describe:\*\* Read technical details

&#x20;  - \*\*Suggest:\*\* Get recommendations

3\. \*\*Enable features\*\* as you need them — everything is \*\*off by default\*\*.



\## Privacy



\- \*\*All your data stays on your device\*\* — SERF doesn't upload anything without your explicit permission.

\- \*\*No accounts required\*\* — Use the app without signing up for anything.

\- \*\*Open source\*\* — The code is public. Anyone can audit it.



\## Get Involved



Want to help build SERF? Check out the project on GitHub:

\[GitHub Repository](https://github.com/yourusername/serf)



\## Version

SERF v0.1.0 (Early Alpha)



\## License

MPL-2.0

14.3 Standing In-App Education Requirement

From State.md + GEMINI.md:



This formalizes something already implied by the Five Lenses (4.14) into an explicit rule:



Every feature's "Describe" lens must be written for someone with zero technical background, not just accurately, but genuinely understandably:



What this does

Why it matters to you

What happens if you turn it on

A user should never have to leave the app to understand what a feature they're looking at actually does.



This is the in-app half of "know what they're downloading". The How-To Guide Library (section 12) is the out-of-app half, for things beyond what any single feature can explain.



Implementation:



Every feature must implement the Five Lenses Standard (4.14)

Explain lens = Plain-language description (what, why, impact)

Describe lens = Technical documentation (how it works, API details)

Suggest lens = Actionable recommendations (with Authority Ladder compliance)

Example:



kotlin

Copy

// For the Firewall feature

fun getExplainText(): String {

&#x20;   return "The firewall blocks apps from connecting to the internet. " +

&#x20;          "This protects your privacy by preventing apps from sending your data " +

&#x20;          "to tracking companies or other third parties."

}



fun getWhyItMatters(): String {

&#x20;   return "Many apps send your data to tracking companies without your knowledge. " +

&#x20;          "Blocking these connections gives you control over your personal information."

}



fun getImpactDescription(): String {

&#x20;   return "Some apps may not work correctly if they require internet access. " +

&#x20;          "You can allow specific apps or domains through the firewall."

}

14.4 Closing Note

From State.md:



Everything in this document —



The mission

The 1000+ features

The architecture

The forks still waiting on a decision

— was built one honest conversation at a time, starting from real frustration and a wish for something better.



It's complete enough to start building.



It's also, deliberately, never finished. Section 14.1's invitation is as real as anything else in this file.



Whoever picks this up next: welcome.



You're now part of the story. The project is yours to extend. Build something you'd actually want to use.

15.1 Standing Architectural Boundary

From State.md:



SERF's traffic interception is, and will only ever be, scoped to the device's own traffic.



Altering what the device's own apps/network stack sees or sends = configuring your own property

Nothing in this register intercepts, alters, or affects another person's traffic on a shared network

This line is structural, not incidental.



This is why:



MAC/IMEI-to-apps spoofing (fine) = local to the device

Spoofing to deceive a cellular network (not fine) = affects third-party systems

The dividing principle:



General-purpose capability over the user's OWN device and OWN data stream is never restricted

Tools whose designed function requires a non-consenting third party are declined

15.2 The Real Dividing Principle (Stated Once, For Every Future Session)

From State.md:



General-purpose capability over the user's OWN device and OWN data stream is never restricted, nannied, or technically gated.



F664's Live Intercept and Edit and the whole universal override engine (F662/F947) already prove this throughout the register

That stays exactly as open as it is

What stays declined is narrower than "anything technically possible":



Only items whose designed function requires a non-consenting third party to exist for the tool to work at all (items 3, 4, 5, 8's redistribution half, 2)

A disclaimer between SERF and the user cannot supply that missing third party's consent, so it doesn't change the legal reality
